<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s philosophical and theological defense of the "Weapon Salve" against the attacks of the parson William Foster. Fludd is explaining how the human soul and the "vital spirit" (the life-force) function beyond the physical body, connecting the blood in a wound to the blood in the healing ointment.</meta>

# FOSTER’S SPONGES <note>original: "Spongia Fosterianæ"; Fludd’s title implies he is "wiping away" the arguments of his opponent, William Foster.</note>

...receives a different name, and for that reason is called by them in one respect **rational**, in another **concupiscible** <note>the part of the soul associated with desires and longings</note>, and in a third **irascible** <note>the part of the soul associated with anger, courage, or the impulse to avoid harm</note>; it is fit to be illuminated, to understand those things which are above itself, below itself, and within and with itself. For by this property or faculty, it knows God above itself; the Angels, who are filled with that very same spirit; and whatever is contained within the whole circle of the heavens. Such is the spiritual act of its central emanation, by reason of that powerful virtue granted to it by God. Through the second and third parts, the soul is inclined either toward desiring and being affected by something, or toward avoiding and fleeing from it—that is, either to love or to hate. And by this property, it exercises itself in **Sympathy and Antipathy** <note>natural attractions and repulsions between things</note> toward those things which are either suitable or discordant to its specific nature. Therefore, in this duty, it works powerfully in and around the effects of this **Weapon Salve** <note>original: "Vnguenti armarij"</note>, since every affection proceeds from those latter two operations: for just as all joy and hope proceed from desire (because nature by its own nature rejoices, etc.), and thus by the union of a vivid emanation, the "death" or frozen spirit in the Ointment is quickened and brought to life. So, on the other hand, the living soul or natural spirit (in which the supernatural spirit acts) is, by its own nature, subject to a kind of spiritual pain and fear because of the weapons that inflicted violence upon it. To this belong the passions, which are perfected by the "irascible" spirit, insofar as it either grieves or is made sorrowful by the violence inflicted, or fears that it may grieve or become sorrowful thereafter. These four affections of the human spirit are the initiators and, as it were, the common subject of all virtuous and vicious actions that happen to man. Now, to express the wide extension of that central spirit which works radically in the vital spirits, the wiser Philosophers assert that it looks upon itself within itself so that it may rightly understand itself. And when it wishes to know God, it raises itself above itself through its mental rays; it penetrates all things and beholds all things, whether present or absent. When it pleases, it travels to places across the sea and scrutinizes our hidden things; indeed, in a moment, it emits and directs its rays to the most remote borders of the whole world and searches out its secrets. It descends into the deep, and from there ascends again into heaven, and clings firmly to Christ, and is made one with Christ.

Is it truly necessary that the infinite power of this all-penetrating spirit, according to **Master Foster’s Dogma**, be determined and limited by some imaginary sphere of activity assigned by the vain philosophy of the Heathens <note>original: "Ethnicorum"; referring to non-Christian or purely materialist Greek philosophers</note>, which, as the Apostle says, is fashioned from the traditions of men and the world, and not of God? Does he not warn us to take care lest we be deceived by that kind of philosophical doctrine which disagrees with the rules of Christ, in whom the fullness of the Divinity dwells bodily? And must we, to obey the fantastical idea of Master Foster, violate the laws of the Apostle and be mocked by his illegitimate philosophy? But, let us return to our purpose.

All this mentioned above having been rightly considered—namely, the **Universal** <note>original: "Catholica"</note> nature of the spirit which breathes life into creatures, the indivisibility and individuality of the Giver and the gift that is given (for the divine essence is indivisible, and therefore the divine spirit imparted to the creature is continuous and undivided from Him who gives it)—and the infinity of its extension, insofar as it is determined by no limits (and for this reason it is said: *the spirit blows where it wills*, and that without obstacle); and that this spirit can turn itself from a vivid and active power into a potential, freezing, and lethal property in a creature by withdrawing its active rays from the circumference of the creature (where it sent them for the sake of living actions) back into the center—that is, into itself—where (in respect to the creature) it rests. Thus, through coagulation, it deprives the spirit of the creature of motion, act, and life, which it previously imparted by its restless activity to the natural spirits to generate blood, fat, flesh, and bones. For this reason, I say—namely, because of the presence of this incorruptible spirit in the blood of the creature—God strictly forbade the Israelites from eating blood: because 
<margin>Leviticus 17</margin>
(as the Text says) **the life of the flesh is in the blood.** Observe here, therefore, that the spirit of life is from God, who gives life to all things: the life of the blood and the fat is in this spirit and from this spirit. Wherefore it stands written elsewhere: 
<margin>Leviticus 3; Acts 17:24</margin>
**You shall not eat blood and fat at all; for the life of the flesh is in the blood, and the life of the bones is in the flesh.** It is easy, therefore, to discern what a concatenation <note>a chain-like connection</note> of members there is here in succession, which derive their life from one and the same radical essence or spirit, and are thence compelled to sympathize with one and the same harmony in the composition of the creature; for from **one blood** He created the whole human race (as Saint Paul says quite fittingly). Consequently, since all flesh and bones are produced from one blood, it is necessary that there be a great relationship between them and human blood in general, and consequently between the blood and the ointment that is made from them.

Having rightly considered and weighed these things as infallible foundations, we conclude in this way: in the proposed question, these five objects must be observed by us: namely, first, the **wound**; second, the **blood** flowing from the wound; third, the **mode of conduction and transfer** from the ointment to the wound, at any distance; fourth, the **nature of the ointment**; and finally, the **mode of operation** by which the cure is completed.

<summary>Robert Fludd argues that the human soul is an extension of God’s indivisible spirit, capable of acting at great distances through "mental rays." He uses Biblical passages from Leviticus and Acts to prove that life resides in the blood, creating a sympathetic link between a patient's wound and the blood-based "Weapon Salve."</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s explanation of the spiritual and physical mechanics of the "Weapon Salve." He is arguing that the vital spirit remains in blood even after it leaves the body, which allows for a sympathetic connection between a patient’s wound and the healing ointment applied to the weapon that caused it.</meta>

Fol. 12.

### EXPRESSION OF THE FIRST PART <note>original: "EXPRESSIO MEMB. I."</note>

C 4

<meta>catchword: its</note>

First, therefore, regarding the wound itself: it is a violation of the work that the **spirit of life** <note>the vital force that animates the body</note> was performing. Specifically, it is an effusion of blood—in which the spirit of life is carried and moved—an obstacle and diversion to the course of the **natural humors** <note>the four primary fluids of the body: blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile</note>, and a division and separation of the fat, flesh, and other similar parts from their integrity and continuity. It is a hindrance and a harm to that peaceful act of life produced by the incorruptible spirit of God, which, by this very property or attribute, is fit for giving life to all things. For this reason, this radical, acting spirit takes an interest in this unnatural business or event, finding its work hindered and its essential action disturbed by the wound or the violence offered to it. For, since the blood is its **vehicle** <note>original: "vehiculum"; the physical carrier of a spiritual force</note>, and its life-giving act was intended to circulate in the organic blood and to produce from there a **transmutation** <note>the process of changing one substance into another, such as food into flesh</note> into flesh and other parts for the sake of growth, multiplication, and the preservation of the individual; now, that same blood is poured out from the mouth of the wound and made useless and of no effect. The body (to whose animation this secret spirit is perpetually and diligently inclined) is weakened and consumed by exhaustion. Therefore, just as incorruptible and life-giving Nature intended to rectify its human spirit through its vivid activity, so—by Hercules!—it is ready and prompt to resist every violence offered, and to correct and restore whatever the violent rupture caused; much like a prudent spider which, when its web is finished and partially damaged, uses all diligence to restore it to its former perfection.

Secondly, although the blood—as the vehicle of the spirit of life—is evacuated from the wound, it nevertheless retains within itself this spirit of life, but in a different property. For it no longer acts toward life—that is, it does not send its rays from the **center to the circumference** <note>Fludd uses the geometry of a circle to describe how life-force radiates outward from the heart/soul to the body’s edges</note> to produce life—but on the contrary, as if burdened and grieving by the violence of the act, it contracts itself from the circumference to the center. That is, it moves from action in the circumference of the creature into itself, having contracted into its center where it seems to rest. And thus it leaves its vehicle, the air, as if congealed, stunned, and dead. And here this mystery is revealed: namely, the reason why, when a murderer is brought before the slain person, the spirit resting centrally in the blood miraculously emanates and flows out, making the blood fluid again. This happens because it is incited and moved by a similar spirit acting and activating **antipathetically** <note>original: "Antipathicè"; a natural repulsion or reaction</note> from the blood of the murderer. For, as I said before, this spirit in its **irascible** <note>the defensive or "angry" faculty of the soul</note> property is just as fit for pursuing with hatred as it is in its **concupiscible** <note>the desiring or "loving" faculty</note> property for loving. <margin>Gen. 9.</margin> And for this reason, the Text teaches us that the blood of the slain is required not only from man but even from a beast, if it was shed by it. And again, the blood of any creature is not to be eaten; this would be merely a superficial rule, were it not that the spirit of life rests in the blood after its effusion. <margin>Levit. 17.</margin> Furthermore, this reasoning—that the blood of animals caught in hunting or fowling ought to be buried in the earth—would otherwise have little validity or efficacy. <margin>Num. 35.</margin> For this reason it is said elsewhere: the land defiled by the blood of the slain cannot be expiated except by the blood of him who shed the other’s blood. Finally, why would it be said that the blood is the seat of the spirit of life, unless the spirit participated with it after it was poured from the wound, congealed and as if dead, resting in its center? Indeed, this spirit altogether leaves and dismisses the blood of the dead, since its life (as stated) is in the blood; yet it does not leave the bones themselves, insofar as they participate in the nature of the most terrestrial part of the flesh. From this it happened that when certain thieves threw the corpse of someone they had murdered into the tomb of **Elisha** <note>the Biblical prophet; the story is found in 2 Kings 13:21</note>, the slain man rose again to life by the mere contact with Elisha’s bones—which could hardly have been achieved unless his divine and life-giving nature had participated with his bones. And this is why it is said that after Elisha’s death his body prophesied, and that in life he performed wonders, and in death his works were miraculous. Finally, the learned and wise Philosophers (speaking **enigmatically** <note>using riddles or symbolic language</note> of this spirit) say that in the body there is a certain small bone called **Luz** <note>term: "Luz"; in Jewish folklore and Hermetic thought, an indestructible bone at the base of the spine from which the body will be resurrected</note>, which will remain with a man until the last day and will cause him to rise again. But these things are to be understood according to their proper sense, not in a common way. Let it be fixed in the memory, I pray, that the contact of Elisha's bones caused the dead to rise from death to life.

In the third place, I come to the manner of the conduction and translation of the blood from the wound to the **Ointment** <note>original: "Unguentum"; the weapon salve</note>. The blood is taken from the living source of that wounded man’s blood—either insofar as it is smeared upon the weapons that inflicted the wound, or insofar as it is bound to a stick, or iron, or some other thing—and thus it is transferred to the ointment at any moderate distance. Now, the reason must be shown how it is possible for any certain relation to exist between the wound and the ointment. For, as **Foster** <note>William Foster, the parson who attacked the use of the weapon salve as witchcraft</note> says, there can be castles, mountains, walls, and thick air between the ointment and the wounded person, which might break or entirely hinder this cure.

Furthermore, we must keep it sharply fixed in memory that we clearly explained in our preceding discourse the excellence of the **animating spirit** <note>original: "spiritus animantis"</note>, in which is every virtue and every property of the **four winds** <note>the vital spirits of the macrocosm/universe</note>. Since it is the spirit of spirits, and the spirit blows where it wills, what—I ask—can hinder its act or operation? And at what distance or interval can its activity...

<summary>Fludd argues that blood contains a vital spirit that persists even after leaving the body, enabling sympathetic healing. He uses the biblical example of Elisha's bones and the folklore of "cruentation" (corpses bleeding near their killers) to prove that life-force remains in physical remains.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s defense of the "Weapon Salve" against the attacks of William Foster. Fludd is expanding on the nature of the "vital spirit"—a divine, universal force that links all things—to explain how a wound can be healed from a distance by treating the blood on the weapon.</meta>

# FOSTER’S SPONGES <note>original: "SPONGIÆ FOSTERIANÆ." The title refers to Fludd "sponging away" or wiping out the arguments made by his opponent, William Foster.</note>

<meta>catchword: which</meta>

its scope cannot be limited, since it is the spirit of the winds and the soul of lightning and the essence of the Sun and the stars of heaven, which, through its animation, cast out their rays peripherally to every corner of the Horizon or Hemisphere. Can this spirit, because it is contained in the blood, not penetrate many hundreds of miles by emanating from its **bloody vehicle** <note>the physical blood that carries the spirit</note>, just as it comes forth from its cloudy Tabernacle in the form (or rather the vehicle) of a lightning flash, or from its solar place in golden rays? Whence it is said, "in the light is the divinity, and in the divinity is the light." Thus says the <margin>Psalm 104</margin> Psalmist: *clothed with light as with a garment* <note>original: "amictus lumine quasi vestimento"</note>. I tell you that this is the one and the same spirit which is in man, and that which works in the winds; and for that reason it was said: *Come, O spirit, from the four winds.* This spirit can by no means be divided into parts; it fills (as Solomon maintains) the whole earth and has established its seat in heaven, and therefore it resides in the body and spirit of man, as has been said. **Esdras** <note>referring to the apocryphal books of Ezra, popular in mystical philosophy</note>, speaking of this spirit, says (as before) that the spirit of God Almighty created all things and searches out all things in the bowels and hidden places of the earth. By these things it appears that this very spirit, by which man breathes, can neither be bounded nor limited in its penetrative and extensive dimension, nor can its progress be hindered by any intermediate obstacle.

Finally, the man who believes in this spirit and depends upon it can perform whatever he desires; for through true knowledge and use of it, the Prophets and Apostles performed miracles, both in healing and in accomplishing matters of greater administration. This spirit, therefore, is called **intellectual** <note>original: "intellectualis"</note> insofar as it makes one understand; **vital** <note>original: "vitalis"</note> in respect to its giving of life—*The spirit of the strong God <margin>Job 9:6, Job 9, Job 11</margin> made me,* says Job, *and the breath of the Almighty gave me life*—and **natural** in respect to growth and multiplication. *Your visitation,* he says elsewhere, *has preserved my spirit.* It acts and flashes out through hidden rays toward that subject of dead blood which is transferred from the man to the **ointment** <note>the healing salve applied to the weapon</note>; in which blood, though cut off, a portion of the spirit is seen to remain, though it ceases from action for a time. Now, the nature of one thing is truly delighted by the nature of another, insofar as both sympathize with each other. Indeed, all are of the same consonance and degree or union in vital love. 

For example: I take two **lutes** <note>original: "testudines," literally "tortoises," a term for lutes due to their shape</note> or **viols**, or any other similar instrument. Let one of these be placed at one end of a table; place the other at the other end. Let a small piece of chaff or straw be placed upon one string of the second lute, which is tuned to *a, la, mi, re* or *d, la, sol, re* <note>these are names for notes in the Guidonian "hexachord" system, used before modern do-re-mi</note>. And then, let the **Gam-ut** <note>the lowest note of the scale</note> of the first lute be struck; the straw will not move even once, because they do not sympathize in one and the same sound and proportion or wandering tone, and for that reason, they have no relation to each other. So too, if blood is transferred to an ointment that is heterogeneous <note>of a different or incompatible nature</note> in the nature of the wounded person, it will accomplish nothing in this cure. 

But truly, if you place the straw upon the *Gam-ut* or *a-re* <note>low notes</note> of the one, and you strike the other in *Gam-ut* or *a-re*—that is, in **unison**—you will immediately see the straw jump and, as it were, dance upon the other string by reason of the redundant vibration or pleasant activity. A similar formal proportion, which sympathetic harmony causes between any strings, acts upon the other through the air. Indeed, this effect will happen even if there are boards placed between them or other obstacles of that kind which might block the direct line of vibration in the air or the medium between the two lutes. 

In this experiment, one may observe that the struck string is most aptly compared to the blood of the wounded man still in his living body. Through a secret emanation or emission—and that by natural inclination and sympathy—it produces in that same tone a secret communication with the quiet and hidden spirit in the congealed blood which is in the ointment (which I compare to the string where the straw rests). Thus, although the string is silent and at rest in itself, nevertheless, by the action of the other string which is actually moved, the quiet string is excited to act by the actual spirit of the struck string out of mere concord. Similarly, in acting, it sends back and leaves behind a **health-giving harmony** to the acting spirit. This spirit approaches its own resting nature just as the tone of the one acting or struck lute approaches that of the other which was not struck. For, as both are only a single spirit (though they seem to differ by intervals, like the strings on two lutes), so similarly those two tones are only one tone (though they seem to differ) and therefore they yield and give forth only one Unison. 

But since one spirit cannot essentially be separated from the other—no more than the Divinity can essentially be divided into parts—so too one tone can hardly be distinguished essentially from the other. Therefore, it vividly extends itself from the starting point to the end point: from the wound to the ointment, namely, by being one continuous spirit. It is not unlike a thread we see stretched from one end of a room to the other. Since, therefore, this spirit requires a spiritual vehicle similar to itself, it is carried as if upon the wings of the wind in the hidden spirit of the blood, which appears as its vehicle. This is no different than how the spirit of the wind is carried through the air, observing no fixed distance, nor being hindered by mountains, woods, or walls from performing and exerting its power. Just as we see the North wind produce frost, ice, and snow in **Lombardy** <note>a region in Northern Italy</note>, even though the high **Alpine mountains**—which reach to the very clouds—are placed in between. But let me offer a more familiar example regarding a grain of wheat, by which...

### EXPRESSION OF SECTION I. Page 13.

which, considered in itself without its mother, the earth, appears to act in no way, for its spirit lies hidden in the center, not working toward the circumference either by way of growth <note>original: "vegetationis"</note> or multiplication. The fountain from which the **vegetable soul** <note>the life-force responsible for growth in plants</note> draws its origin through multiplication is the **celestial sun**, which works life in all growing things by the power of the aforementioned **Universal Spirit** <note>original: "spiritus Catholici"</note>, which "placed its Tabernacle in the sun" <note>A reference to Psalm 19:4, suggesting the sun is the physical vessel for God's life-giving spirit</note>, giving natural increase of life and growth to every thing. For even if this Spirit is in itself Universal, nevertheless, as it enters into any specific creature, it converts its property toward the quickening, multiplication, and generation of that very species—indeed, even to the human race itself. Whence **Aristotle** says: *The sun and man beget man.* For example: [life] is multiplied by the successive influence of its penetrating spirit in a grain of wheat, which is raised up (as **St. Paul** holds) after death and rot in its own earth, from one grain to twenty; and then afterward it is moved upward in its airy vehicle, with its straw-like stalk toward the fountain of its essence, and it attracts its like from above through the medium of the Universal air by a certain sympathetic or **Magnetic power**. 

But it has been observed by rustics and farmers that the better and more fertile the ground or earth is in its temperament in which the grain is sown, and the closer it is to the nature of the grain, the more the grain will prosper and multiply in power. Now indeed, the fountain of the grain's life—namely the Universal spirit of growth—resides chiefly in the sun of the **macrocosm** <note>the "greater world" or universe</note>, compared to the heart of man or the **microcosm** <note>the "lesser world," the human being</note>, which is the principle of life. The grain is not unsuitably compared to the **physician of the blood**, which is gathered from the bloody tree of life, moving in the veins and arteries as if in a straw stalk or husk. The stalk, always growing up over it with the remaining grains, refers to the whole mass of blood in the veins, which remains in manifest action. The harvested grain [is compared] to the harvested blood; because of this, although both remain without any manifest action or life, they nonetheless possess the spirit of life and multiplication contracted centrally within themselves. And therefore, there remains in them only the potential for acting; but nothing acts as of yet, unless it is called forth and reduced into action by an active, similar, and quickening nature—or rather, by that same continued spirit, to its grain from the sun, or to the harvested blood from the spirit in the wounded body. The internal invisible Spirit of the blood, in which the Spirit of life moves toward the ointment from the wounded person, is compared to the ethereal or celestial Spirit, in which the influence of the incorruptible Spirit moves from the sun downward even to the grain through the common medium or vehicle of both. In which the **Ethereal spirit** moves similarly from the sun downward toward its like, or rather excites itself in the grain now buried in the earth, or from the fountain of life to the deadened grain or the blood in the Ointment, which medium is the common Element of air. The Ointment is the fertile earth in which the bloody grain dies and rises again, about which I shall now speak.

The fourth thing that must be considered here is the ointment and its nature. Who, even a mere simpleton, can deny that like seeks its like, or that one nature cherishes a stronger one, sustains and recreates a weaker one, and that the weaker rejoices in help and receives comfort? The **ancient physicians and Philosophers** observed that lungs nourish lungs, and little brains <note>original: "cerebella"</note> cherish a weakened brain, the spleen helps to comfort the spleen, and for weakened intestines we inject enemas made from a decoction of intestines; the stomach of a rooster helps digestion. The very sputum ejected by a consumptive <note>one suffering from "phthisis" or tuberculosis</note> is said to heal the lungs; likewise, dead worms, reduced to a dry powder, kill intestinal worms. A stone of the bladder or kidneys, prepared in the proper way, cures the stone.

Finally, it is certain that like acts by a natural inclination upon its like: for "nature delights in its own nature" and "nature rejoices in nature." Now indeed, if we look at the composition of this medicine, we will find it to be of **admirable harmony** with human blood; for, as I indicated to you before, the blood is surely the seat of the Spirit of life, and the life of the flesh resides in the blood; and similarly, the Spirit of life is immediately in the fat as much as in the blood. Because of this, it was forbidden that these two be eaten, but they were to be distinctly reserved as a burnt offering due to the Lord. And since the life of the bones is in the blood and the flesh, they therefore participate in the spirit of life and consequently possess within themselves a **Balsamic marrow**, which, filled with spirits, totally affects the remaining parts. Wherefore, without a doubt, there is one and the same relationship of union between this ointment (with the blood in it) and the nature of the wounded person. It is like the relationship between the string of one lute <note>original: "testudinis"</note> which is tuned to another in the same tone: for this reason, it will be ready for the vibration of a mutual agreement of **sympathetic harmony**, if the spirits of both—through the virtual contact of one nature with the other—become [united] through the transfer of individual spirits of one into the body of the other, so that the vivid Balsamic virtue of the one may strengthen and excite the dull and lethal faintness of the other. Just as the activity of one struck lute string excites another to move, which before was quiet and devoid of life; or, as we see, a grain of wheat...

# FOSTER’S SPONGES

...cast into the earth properly and well-mixed with the dung of horses, who themselves were fed on that same grain, it is as quickly as possible brought to life by the rays of the sun, and made mobile and fit to ascend toward the fountain of its acting spirit: For any spirit, by natural instinct or inclination, tends upward to its native region.

Finally: Now I must come to the measure of this mystical healing operation; Foster says that it cannot be completed nor performed by any virtual contact <note>healing through a "virtue" or power that travels across space without physical touching</note>, since it is outside the determined sphere of activity. But does he, or his most sharp-witted master, know the certain and determined limits of activity in any thing, that he concludes so boldly? **"Happy is he who could know the causes of things"** <note>original: "Felix, qui potuit rerum cognofcere causas" — a famous line from the Roman poet Virgil</note>: But I hold for certain that I cannot discern any such happiness in his revelations and prescriptions of limits to natural agents, much less to this spirit, which acts and works "all in all and over all": Who does whatever He wills, as much in the powers of heaven as in the inhabitants of the earth. If this great antagonist of the **weapon-salve** <term>unguenti armarii: a medicinal ointment applied to the weapon that caused a wound, rather than the wound itself, believed to heal by magnetic sympathy</term> would only consider with himself the wonderful operation which this **Universal spirit** <note>original: "Catholicus hic spiritus"</note> produces in this cold and contractive faculty; so that, when it moves from the north and makes snow, frost, and ice by the contraction of the thin **spirit of the world** <note>original: "spiritus mundani"</note> into a thick body, and absorbs the fountains of the earth from above; all of which is accomplished by contracting its action from the circumference to the center, making the common Element change from a dilated spirit to a contracted body, and vice versa from a contracted body to a dilated one: For by some alteration, entirely opposite to this Northern act and northern disposition, it dissolves in its dilating property and resolves whatever it produced through its cold condition in moving and reviving the frozen waters, rendering them translucent, transparent, and spiritual or invisible things, even though before they were thick, opaque, dark, corporeal, and visible.

And again, if he would weigh and consider with his sharp judgment how [the spirit] casts down and depresses the fountains into the earth by its presence in its **solar Tabernacle** <note>The Sun, viewed as the physical dwelling place of God's life-giving spirit</note>, which by its cold property were budding and excited out of the earth: If, I say, he would properly weigh and consider with himself how the sun, now existing in the south beyond the **equinoctial** <note>the equator</note>, refines the thick and heavy air there and dissolves the frozen, snowy, and icy effects which the cold had introduced into that Hemisphere, while the sun's presence in the northern world was working contrary effects; and how on this side of the Equator, by its contractive faculty, it partly sucks up and absorbs the fountains from the Southern world, out of the earth, and partly by its dilating action depresses the said fountains from that side, appearing in the northern climates: Then surely he would not call into question or doubt the action of this most powerful spirit, nor would he limit it (according to the fantastic opinion of some) within an imaginary sphere of activity: Since this spirit is from Him who fills all things, and works all things and in all things: And therefore, consequently, it performs the magnificent works of contraction and dilation, which appear so clearly in every corner of the world. Will he circumscribe this action of the rising of fountains and their reciprocal mutual depression within any sphere or orb, unless it be that of the round world? Will he think that this action—effected between the spiritual state of privation or cold, and that of life and position, which is heat—requires and demands only a tiny interval, so that the extremes are as far apart from each other as North from South, in order to meet and concur in a **symphonic proportion**? The hotter and more immoderate—and consequently more dilated—one hemisphere is, the colder and more contracted is the opposite: And therefore, the greater the depression of the fountains is there because of the intense heat in the other Hemisphere, the more they are sucked out and attracted upward from the earth by the attractive power of the intense cold of the other. This I can and will be ready to prove and demonstrate to anyone who wavers in doubt on this point, by ocular proof or demonstration.

Wherefore, from these things we have brought forward, it is evident that this **Magnetic** way of healing is a Gift of God, according to the opinion of **Paracelsus** <note>The famous 16th-century Swiss physician and alchemist</note>; and not indeed an act of the Devil, as our Foster has published to the world (scarcely in a Christian manner, truly), attributing and ascribing it against reason and conscience to the Devil, the worst and most foul of spirits (whose duty is only destructive and wounding, not indeed constructive or healing) which is the sole property of this best, most beautiful, and purest of all spirits, whom all good Angels serve to fulfill his will, just as the Devil has his malignant Angels as destroyers.

Therefore from these things it is to be seen (kind reader) how life is inspired into the creature by God's good spirit of life: How its seat or vehicle, in which it moves, is the blood; how that flesh, fat, and bones have their life and growth in the spirit, which moves in the blood; how this spirit works privatively, by contracting its rays of life from the circumference to the creature’s center, where it rests, or rather ceases to work the effects of its office of life, as is clearly seen in death and frozen blood or a grain of wheat: And again it works positively toward life, by which it quickens what was dead before, sending out its act from the center to the circumference of the creature, as in a grain of whe-

He concludes that this healing power is a divine gift from God rather than a work of the Devil, as his opponent William Foster claimed.</summary>

# EXPRESSION OF THE FIRST PART

...than a grain of wheat buried in the earth, or in congealed blood adhering either to a staff or to weapons to which the ointment has been transferred, as if to its most natural soil. I have also shown you how the Spirit is one, the same, and indivisible: And therefore, that which resides in the ointment and that which works in the body are essentially linked and continued with one another, insofar as they are of one spirit, even though it may proceed from the "four winds" <note>A biblical and alchemical reference to the four cardinal directions or elements</note>: in essence, I say, it is not divided, but differs only in its properties. For it works by contraction through cold and by dilation through heat. In a similar way, there is only a single common vehicle that carries this spirit in the ethereal substance of the blood. Finally, because the ointment is composed of human blood, human fat, human flesh or **Mummy** <note>powdered remains of embalmed human bodies, a common 17th-century medicine</note>, and the smoky outgrowth of human bones called <term>Usnea</term> or <term>Moss</term> <note>original: "Vsnea seu Muscus" — a specific moss found growing on the skulls of criminals left on gibbets, highly prized in sympathetic medicine</note> which grows from the skull according to my recipe; and because the nature of the **Universal Spirit** <note>original: "Catholici spiritus" — here meaning 'universal' or 'all-pervading' rather than the religious denomination</note> is thus specified in the ointment, even if it does not act [immediately]; it is prioritized to act through the union it now possesses from the rays of the living and operative spirit of the wounded man. This is no different than how the sun works upon the earth, in which the dead grain of wheat lies hidden, as soon as it calls forth and excites the hidden central spirit in the dead blood to work in a similar way by a solar ray in the "atom of life," which is the grain. Wherefore, the mixing of both these now-working spirits in one life-giving union causes them to tend toward the fountain of life. Just as a grain rising from the earth carries its like—which was rejoicing in the earth—upward toward its native region (unless the heavy coverings of the Elements hindered its further ascent). But since this "earth" or ointment is more spiritual, it sends its power to the blood by that harmony which it effects through the continuity of the spirits—namely, as if by a certain union, by reason of the uniformity of the specific spirit regarding man. By this union, the four discordant Elements and every member of the human body are united in a sympathetic harmony for the use of the life adopted in the creature. 

Indeed, insofar as the blood, flesh, fat, and bones in all other brute animals are formed from one kind of Elemental form and are similarly shaped by the same working spirit, it is no wonder if their blood, added to the same Ointment, also causes and brings forth health in a wounded creature. For it generally tends toward life, which is proper to all creatures with blood, making no exception for any specific difference. Whether this, therefore (when weighed rightly and accurately by a jealous rival or even a mediocre intellect), can appear to be an act of the Devil and not a blessed Gift of God, I leave to the rumination and judgment of those who know how to judge more fruitfully than I.

Now indeed (candid reader), so that I may at last conclude this Philosophical part, it is my intention—with a better taste in the savor of the palates of your mature judgments and well-seasoned concepts—to depart from this theoretical and speculative course of demonstration and betake myself for a time to a more practical or experimental mode of direction. By this, you may more conveniently enter and stand upon the plain and the tracks of direct truth regarding the resolution of that aforementioned question. And indeed, I shall divide this practical discourse of mine into three distinct chapters: the first of which will instruct you by a notable and remarkable experience that the agent or internal principle in this cure is in the blood or body of the living and wounded man, and consequently, the Devil must necessarily be excluded from being any agent or actor in it. The second will express various true histories of this cure as they have been performed among us here in England. The third will open the true foundation of why our antagonist and censor <note>William Foster</note> published this book to the public. And the last will report a certain method of sympathetic cures which will appear much more foreign and wonderful, exceeding the grasp of our **Sponge-bearer** <note>Fludd's nickname for Foster, who wrote a book titled "The Sponge" to wipe away the weapon-salve</note> more than that of the <term>Weapon-Salve</term> <note>original: "Vnguentis armarij"</note>. Concerning these things, therefore, in this order.

# THE SECOND PART
## CHAPTER I

->*Here some practical observations regarding this cure are noted,*<-
->*by which it is made manifest that the external agent in this cure is centrally*<-
->*contained in the blood: And consequently, Mr. Foster’s Devil*<-
->*must be excluded from this health-bringing business.*<-

If it were so (as it pleases Mr. Foster) that the Devil alone were the author and actor in this cure, and that he performed it shrewdly and sophistically to deceive those simple traveling physicians, then it is not likely that there could be any agent in the blood that could resist his cure or hinder it. This appears even more probable because the nature of the wounded person is fit and ready to lay aside all weakness according to its strength, and desires to possess its Tabernacle <note>the body</note> in a healthy state. And for this reason, one can see that by the application of ointments or balms or internal medicines, the natural spirits and the internal actor of life are helped...

He argues that the healing process is a natural, God-given harmony between the spirit in the blood and the ointment, setting the stage to prove Foster's "Devil" theory wrong through practical examples.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s defense of the "weapon-salve," a sympathetic remedy where the medicine is applied to the weapon that caused a wound rather than the wound itself. In the preceding text, Fludd argued that the Universal Spirit connects the blood on the weapon to the blood in the patient’s body. Here, he provides practical evidence and personal anecdotes to prove that this healing power is a natural force of the "life spirit" rather than a trick of the Devil.</meta>

# FOSTER’S SPONGES
<meta>catchword: now</meta>

to act and promote medicines in their healings; otherwise, they would scarcely achieve anything of the sort. For this reason, the physician is commonly called <term>nature’s helper</term> <note>original: "medicus adjutor naturæ"</note>.

Now, I shall prove in the following way that the primary agent of this form of healing is contained within the body of the wounded person: This experiment has been tested and found to be true through daily experience by men of distinguished learning—not by "sellers of smoke" or charlatans <note>original: "puppi aut fumi venditoribus" — a phrase for frauds who sell empty promises</note>, but by men of far greater maturity in both doctrine and judgment than our "sponge-bearer" <note>A jab at William Foster, the author of "The Hop-locrisma," whom Fludd is refuting</note>. They have found that when a wounded man has placed his blood upon a staff, a piece of iron, or a weapon for the application of the Ointment, and is moreover on the path to recovery, if during that time he should have sexual relations with a menstruating woman, the curative power in the ointment is immediately destroyed and will no longer benefit him. Similarly, they have often observed that if the wounded man happens to suffer a nosebleed during this time, this curative property is entirely taken away and will provide no further help. From these examples, it appears that this happens because the secret action and emanation of the internal <term>life spirit</term> <note>original: "spiritus vitæ"</note> in the wounded man is diverted and distracted from its focus or "aspect" toward the <term>weapon-salve</term> <note>original: "Unguentum armarium"</note>. Instead, it sends out another emanation to work elsewhere, which is so immediate to itself and its body that—just as we see in the heavens—a greater "aspect" or more powerful alignment of one planet toward another can overwhelm and remove a lesser one.

Another remarkable experiment was proven by a man of the highest nobility, whom I shall mention at greater length in the sixth chapter of this section. One of his servants severely wounded his finger with a scythe—right at the joint—while he was mowing grass. His finger bled continuously, and it could not be easily stopped. Therefore, this nobleman ordered that the scythe be knocked out of its handle and brought to him so that he might grease it. As the wounded man himself knocked the blade out at the first stroke or blow to the tool that had inflicted the wound, the blood was stopped, and he did not shed another drop thereafter. And indeed, the nobleman acknowledged that even if not a single drop of blood could be seen or discerned upon those weapons, if he nevertheless smeared the spot on the instrument that had struck the wound (which he confessed he was often forced to do by guesswork), he achieved the cure just as effectively as if the blood had remained upon it. From this revelation or discovery, I gather that the whole mystery of this cure consists in the secret and invisible spirit which is in the blood, remaining as much at rest and at work in the wounded body as in that which penetrated invisibly into the weapon. For otherwise, without the presence of visible blood, it could not work. Indeed, from this observation—namely, that the blood stopped instantly when the scythe or weapon was struck—a mystery is revealed just as great as when the presence of a murderer causes the congealed blood in a slain man to flow again <note>This refers to "cruentation," the folklore belief that a corpse's wounds bleed in the presence of the killer</note>. All of this is effected partly by the contractive property of the hidden spirit in the blood and partly by its dilation, as was said before.

From this, it is clear that it is not the Devil who performs anything by external operation in this cure, but that it arises from the central emanation of that spirit in the wounded man which grants him life, and which works from eternity, as is clearly demonstrated by these experiments. I now come to the following chapter on empirical healings.

### CHAPTER II.

->In which several accounts concerning the effect of this kind of healing are expressed.<-

Now, I shall relate to you the histories of several domestic healings performed by this weapon-salve, so that wise men may sincerely judge from them whether the Devil has any right in this method of healing or not.

There is a Knight living in Kent, a man of excellent judgment, religion, and learning, named Sir Nicolas Gilbourne; a man, I say, with whom I am and have been most familiar, for he took my sister as his wife. This Knight was very close with a certain Captain named Stiles, who in former years was his tenant or fellow-citizen. He spent time with the said Captain in the company of upright and learned theologians at the time this ointment was being prepared. They saw all the ingredients individually, and afterward, he ordered the apothecary to compose them without any appearance of superstitious action. It was generally determined there to be a lawful medicine and in no way diabolical. A jar full of this ointment was given as a gift to my brother <note>Likely referring to his brother-in-law, Gilbourne</note>. What healthy effects it performed, I shall relate to you (as they say) in three words, and that verbatim, just as I possess it in his own handwriting.

First (he says), it was at Chatham in Kent, where a servant of a certain shipwright named Poppe was wounded by an axe-stroke so deep that nothing could be more so, yet the hand was not entirely cut off. After this injury (which was in the afternoon), he was brought to me, but I refused to have anything to do with him; I only advised that he wash the wound with his own urine, which he did. At first light the following morning, I greased the axe, and after greasing it, I sent someone to the said man to inquire how he was doing. The reply was that he had been in the greatest torments through the whole night, but...

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s defense of "magnetic" or "sympathetic" healing. Fludd is arguing against William Foster, who claimed these cures were the work of the Devil. Fludd provides three specific case studies—testified by Sir Nicholas Gilbourne and Captain Stiles—to prove that the cure works through a natural, spiritual connection between the blood on a weapon and the patient’s body, even across great distances.</meta>

# DESCRIPTION OF PART I

...that not long ago he had sent them back. The following morning, entering my <term>study</term> <note>original: "Musæum"</note>, I threw my sword upon my axe; the hilt of the sword knocked the <term>weapon salve</term> <note>original: "unguentum," the sympathetic ointment applied to the weapon rather than the wound</note> off the axe. When I noticed this, I sent another person to find out how the patient was doing then: and I received the answer that he had felt very well all through the night, but this morning he was tormented by the greatest pains and his <note>original: "ira," literally "anger," here referring to the raging inflammation or "fury" of the wound</note> inflammation persisted. Therefore, having smeared the axe a second time, I sent word to him, and I perceived that he felt the greatest relief; within seven days he was perfectly restored to health.

These are his very words, which he gave to me in letters. By these, it is clear that this cure (contrary to Mr. Foster’s assertion) is performed by the weapon salve, and truly not by any other secret medicine applied by the Devil. Rather, this "diabolical invention" belongs to Mr. Foster, while the cure of the ointment is natural. For otherwise, how could the Ointment adhering to the axe—once uncovered or knocked off by the sword’s hilt—be the cause of a sudden change in the wound from better to worse? And how would the wound return from its painful state to its former, relaxed condition after a fresh and second anointing of the weapon or instrument?

The second story of this way of curing was this: "I," says Sir Nicholas Gilbourne, "had undertaken the draining and fishing of a certain pond at Charing. When I had finished the task, the town boys entered the waters to search for fish. Among the others, Brent Deering (son of Master Finch Deering) entered for the same purpose, and there a reed penetrated through his calf. It was dripping blood heavily and twisting him with the greatest pains, which was the reason his mother sent for a certain Surgeon, John Hart of Charing, to examine the wound and bind it up. But the boy continued, after the binding, to be burdened with the highest tortures and was ready to faint. Therefore his sister was sent to me, so that I might do what I could for his relief. I replied that I could provide no good because he had already been bound up by the Surgeon, but this would not satisfy them. Therefore, because of their persistence, I advised them (because they told me the opening of the wound was very narrow) that they should wash away all the Surgeon’s smearing, and that they should insert a weaver’s or wool-worker’s needle into the wound as far as it would penetrate, with a thread tied to it so it would not go further. Doing so, they found it penetrated all the way to the skin of the opposite side. I smeared this needle with the salve, and within four or five days it was apparently healed, except that there was a certain dry <term>scab</term> <note>original: "psora"</note> at the top of the opening. I was not at all satisfied, because I found it had not recovered entirely, but the scab caused a delay. Therefore, I smeared the needle anew toward night, and the following morning a small splinter of the reed came out of the opening; afterward, within two or three days, it was healed in its entirety."

These are the very words of Sir Nicholas Gilbourne’s letter. What will our Foster say to this cure? What? Did the Devil truly perform this cure through other medicines, and not through this sympathetic or Magnetic Ointment? The matter is indeed manifest: the entire curative power consists in the Ointment. For the effects in curing testified as much; but the principal agent proceeded, or rather emanated, from the wounded body.

And alas! What could the Devil gain from a poor boy by providing this secret way of curing, when the boy sought nothing but the assistance of God’s blessing and beneficence for his relief? <margin>Psalm 9:10</margin> An innocent little boy, I say, and therefore under the protection of the Almighty. "A refuge for the righteous," says David, "is God, and a bulwark." <margin>Matthew 18:11</margin> What? Was the guardian Angel of this little boy (who, as Christ says, always looks upon and beholds the face of his Father, who is in heaven) so negligent that he would lose and forfeit the special command committed to him by God, through such wretched devilish craftiness? <margin>Psalm 34</margin> "The Angels camp," says the Psalmist, "around those who fear him, and he receives them." Or will they also be so easily deluded—they who are more vigilant in preserving God’s elect than the hundred-eyed <note>A giant from Greek mythology who had one hundred eyes, making him the ultimate watchman</note> Argus?

From these things, one may discern the folly of our <term>sponge-bearer</term> <note>Fludd’s mocking nickname for William Foster, who wrote "The Squeezing of Paracelsus' Spunge"</note> and his imaginary fictions regarding the profound mystery of this way of curing. I come to the third case.

Near Windsor, there was someone who had some business in the woods. While he was mowing a part of a meadow, he fell backward onto the point of his scythe, and tore his whole back so ruinously that his life was called into doubt. The scythe was immediately sent to London to Captain Stiles, who smeared it, wrapped it up, and put it away. A little while after, someone came inquiring about <note>Likely Captain Stiles himself, or a physician in his household</note> Doctor Stiles and was sent to him. A minister, understanding the man was there to give thanks for the cure performed by the weapon salve, sent him to the Captain, who wanted to see him for a few moments. The Captain was at dinner or supper with several of his friends and sent for the man into the dining room. There, the man reported to the Captain that the wounded person acknowledged his life was saved by the Captain, next after God. He informed him that the dangerous wound began closing without delay after the scythe had been sent to him, and he was healed in total without any other application. In recompense for this, and as a token of a grateful heart, he gave the Captain a side of venison.

Here one may see that this cure was performed across a distance of 20 miles between the wound and the ointment.

# FOSTER’S SPONGE.
<meta>catchword: then</meta>

### CHAPTER III.

->*Here the worldly craftiness and fraud, or political maneuvering, of our sponge-bearer <note>Fludd refers to William Foster as the "sponge-bearer," mocking the title of Foster’s book, "The Hoplocrisma-Spongus," which Foster intended to use to "wipe away" the weapon-salve.</note> in attacking and publicizing the Weapon-Salve—cloaked under a polished veil of sanctity—is detected and brought to light.*<-

But, insofar as the experiments cited and proved by Captain Stiles and Sir Nicholas Gilbourne are only two or three, and according to the old saying, "one or two examples do not prove an argument"; it does not seem absurd to me to assure any reader that there is a certain nobleman of this kingdom—excellent in religion, judgment, and learning—who at the very first threshold hissed at this kind of cure as an impossible thing. But after he discovered it to be truly real, he was frightened by such jackdaws <note>original: "graculis," a term of derision for noisy, foolish talkers</note> as Mr. Foster, who would prevent it from being put into action and use: insofar as he was persuaded that there was some deceptive trickery or <term>Cacomagical</term> <note>From the Greek 'kakos' (evil) and 'magia' (magic); refers to "black magic" or diabolical sorcery.</note> operation and power in it. For this reason, he abstained and desisted from its use and practice, although he acknowledged its action was marvelous. And yet nonetheless, because his curiosity incited him to plunge a little deeper into the truth, toward the end he had a mind to call upon Captain Stiles (a man well known to him through intimacy to be both wise and pious, and also opposed to all deceptive or superstitious actions and ceremonies). Since it was known that he [Stiles] was a distinguished practitioner of this way of healing, the nobleman's greedy desire for his own better satisfaction was first to survey with his own eyes each particular ingredient that went into the composition of this Ointment, and afterward to observe the preparation of each ingredient, and finally to watch and perceive their mixing or union in the composition, so that he might better discern whether any illicit act or Diabolical superstition concurred with its making. The Captain agreed; moreover, this nobleman, so that he might be better informed in this business, ordered a divine or learned theologian to be summoned to witness this composition, along with a famous Doctor of Medicine. At the end of the work, they all with one consent affirmed that there was no harmful superstition in the making of the ointment, as was falsely pretended, nor even any <term>Cacomagical</term> disposition in the ingredients, as was vainly pretended and alleged by certain <term>precise</term> <note>A term often used for Puritans or those considered overly scrupulous and narrow-minded in their religious views.</note> men. And for this reason, they unanimously concluded that both the medicine in itself and the practice of this way of healing were natural and consequently lawful for any Christian to use. Hence this honorable man for the space of a year exercised this way of healing upon many who were wounded, with happy and prosperous success. And yet, notwithstanding these things, it was likely that some meddling buzzards or buzzing flies of this nature had introduced him to new opinions, insinuating to him that the Captain might use certain superstitious means, or unknown tricks, in collecting and preparing the principal ingredients, which he himself could hardly discern, and that without this, those mystical effects could hardly be performed. Thereupon, after a year had passed, the said nobleman, for greater certainty, prepared to concoct the composition himself, and to have at hand the ingredients acquired and prepared under his own direction—namely, <term>Usnea</term> <note>A type of moss or lichen, specifically that which grows on the skull of a man who died a violent death; it was a prized ingredient in Paracelsian medicine.</note> from human bones, etc. And for this reason, he appointed Mr. Cooke as his apothecary, to grind the ingredients into powder, and afterward to compose and store them. When he had performed this, he found that this composition of his own possessed the same curative power, and he rested so confidently in the certainty and lawfulness of this mode of cure, that not even any of the said "fantastic butterflies" could change his mind or turn him away from this practice by their painted demonstrations devoid of solid substance. When our giant-like sponge-bearer [Foster] perceived this—finding that all the vain persuasions of his overly scrupulous and suspicious faction could achieve nothing—he himself, like a herald or an orator crying out for the rest, brings forth these fragments of his external and painted zeal, insofar as it is stuffed inside and out with hypocritical and political intent, as will be explained more widely hereafter.

---

->His words in the Dedicatory Epistle are these.<-

> That which compelled me to publish this little book was commiseration for the state of certain men of good reputation, esteemed as pious, who use the Weapon-Salve: I pity them; I believe they suspect no loss or harm in it. I pray and beseech for them in the very words of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ: *Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do.* <note>Foster is quoting the Gospel of Luke (23:34) to imply the users of the salve are committing a grave sin out of ignorance.</note>

But is this not an extraordinary mask of zeal and religion, smeared over with the makeup of dissimulation? Through a polished covering of the face, I say...

He then quotes William Foster’s condescending dedication, which compares users of the salve to the crucifiers of Christ.</summary>

# THE EXPOSITION OF MEMBER I. Fol. 16

...then of hypocrisy, used to cover the nakedness of his own worldly craftiness—namely, to wrongly and foolishly apply and falsely use the just and pious words of our blessed Savior Christ Jesus for the decrying and abolition of the benefit of that gift of healing. This gift is effected through His life-giving spirit upon these wounded and weak creatures; and (what is worse) he falsely attributes that power to the Devil, the enemy of both God and man. Thus, the Creator and actor of all things is defrauded of His right, and that in the sight of all His rational creatures, whom He created in His own image, by persuading them to believe that all mystical and hidden healing in these last days is the virtuous operation of the Father of Lies and the enemy of goodness.

And he does this especially for the sake of the Surgical society <note>original: "societatis Chirurgicæ." Fludd suggests Foster is acting as a lobbyist for the Company of Barber-Surgeons, who viewed the "Weapon-Salve" as a threat to their monopoly on wound care.</note>, as will soon be more clearly declared—as if he were saying that God, in this last age of the world, had lost all operative virtue and power in mystical and hidden operations, whether in His creatures or in miracles. Has this good God (I say), the Creator of both heaven and earth, who for that reason works miracles forever, both hiddenly and manifestly, either lost His operative virtue, or assigned it as if by inheritance to the Devil? Would not the aforementioned nobleman and many other pious and religious men, at whom Mr. Foster aims in the preceding text, retort with a better conscience his aforementioned speech, saying:

> I am moved with compassion for this Priest—or one held to be a Priest by his religious profession—who condemns and strives to erase with his "Sponge" <note>A reference to Foster's book, the *Hoplocrisma-Spongus*, which Foster claimed would "wipe away" the error of the weapon-salve like a sponge.</note> and root out the weapon-ointment <note>original: "unguentum armarium"</note> and abolish the reputation of that virtue which God has bestowed upon it for the good of man and the relief of the wretched wounded. I pity him; if he does this out of ignorance, then I think he suspects no evil. And therefore,
<margin>Luke 22</margin>
> I will pour out prayers for him in the very words of our Savior: *Father, forgive him; for he knows not what he does.* <note>original: "Pater, ignosce illi: nescit enim quid agat."</note> But if he does this knowingly and craftily, and uses Christ's words for the sake of cunning, or to procure for himself worldly gain from others, I must address him in these words which Christ gave to Peter: *Get behind me, Satan; for you do not understand the things that are of God, but those things*
<margin>Mark 8:33</margin>
> *that are of men.* <note>The transcription lists Mark 8:22, but the quote is from verse 33.</note>

Now, by Hercules! <note>original: "Herclé," a common exclamation used to emphasize the truth of a statement.</note> I shall make it manifest that he covers human craftiness with a cloak of holiness, and consequently involves himself more deeply in the mud of sin; therefore, it is necessary that his retaliation should justly correspond to his merit. For God, who searches the hearts and kidneys <note>original: "renes." In biblical language, the kidneys or "reins" were considered the seat of a person's deepest emotions and intentions.</note> of every man, is just and pays back justly. I would therefore wish every attentive reader to note that there is a double reason in Mr. Foster's effort to destroy the Weapon-Ointment: the first of which is the weakest, namely, a crafty ignorance or total disbelief in those invisible virtues of God's creatures, even though the effect clearly appears to the glory of God and the solace of wretched men. From this arises the captious disposition of some "precisians" <note>original: "præcisionum," a term often used to describe those with overly rigid or Puritanical religious views.</note>, or rather men who only appear to be so, who place no faith or trust in hidden and secret operations, whether of medicines or of any other thing manifested in these last ages. For, they say, miracles have ceased; and therefore, anything that now appears miraculous or worthy of admiration is deceptive, <term>Cacomagical</term> <note>From the Greek 'kakos' (evil) and 'magia' (magic); it refers to black magic or sorcery performed through the aid of evil spirits.</note>, diabolical, and consequently not from God at all. 

And this kind of man indeed forgets the words of the Wise Man, who
<margin>Ecclesiastes 11</margin>
tells us: *The works of the Lord are wonderful and glorious; His works are secret and unknown among men,* etc. And will these unjust judges dare to disapprove of the action of God, and condemn them, and attribute them to the Devil, because they are secret and unknown to themselves? Shall they tax those things which far exceed their grasp? May I not justly say to those men who, in their writings, strike against the truth, as Peter said to Ananias? Why
<margin>Acts 3 & 5</margin>
has Satan filled your heart, that you should lie to the Holy Spirit, and unjustly apply the power of God to the devil?

But, if he replies and says that he knows for certain that this secret and hidden power is not from God, but from the Devil: I ask, with what audacity do we clothe ourselves to ask of him how he arrived at that knowledge? Or by what means did he acquire for himself that familiarity with the Devil, so that the Devil is so obligated to him as to let him know his secrets? As for those things of God, I know they are far enough from the grasp of such men for them to seek a reason from them. And for this reason Solomon says, *I understood that of all the works*
<margin>Ecclesiastes 8:17</margin>
*of God, man can find no reason for those things that are under the sun; and the more he labors to seek it out, the less he will find.* And will this bold race of men, like blind horses (or according to the English proverb, <term>Blind Bayards</term> <note>A "Bayard" was a famous horse from medieval folklore. To be a "Blind Bayard" was to be someone who, though ignorant, acts with extreme and foolish boldness.</note>), dare to assert that the hidden and concealed virtue in God's creatures is effected by the Devil?

If it is indeed entirely so—namely, that it is impossible to judge these hidden mysteries and concealed actions of God except by divining—then indeed I would like to know if any learned reader could be so sluggish as to affirm that Mr. Foster and his followers or assistants acted more rightly by attributing the reason for the secret method of healing by the Weapon-Ointment to the Devil (having no certain testimony from the sacred page), than Doctor Fludd, who ascribes all goodness—and among other things, the action of healing in general—to God and His blessed Word.

From this kind of sharp and keen-scented...
<meta>catchword: nose</meta>

Fludd contends that healing is a divine gift and that Foster’s claim—that God no longer performs "secret" miracles—is a limit on God’s eternal power.</summary>

# FOSTER’S SPONGES.

...of men and of agile and audacious judges, who give their counsel against God's cause loosely and at the toss of a die; indeed, they would even try to peremptorily persuade the common people that they are sharers in all of God's mysteries—things which have not been revealed to the human race, but are known by the outcome alone. From among these, I say, who assists Mr. Foster in this "Glorious" undertaking? They are like the Titanic Giants, prompt and ready to stand against and insult Jove <note>original: "Iovi," referring to Jupiter/God</note>, for the sake of Saturn, or rather, for the sake of Satan. It is these very people who, with the spurs of their own great confidence and self-assurance, provoke him to rail against the power of God in the **Weapon-Salve** <note>original: "Unguento armario," a medicinal ointment that, according to "magnetic" theory, healed a wound when applied to the weapon that caused it rather than the body.</note>; and they tear me and many others apart with insults for defending the justice of God and His created nature. Of these men, I say, he makes mention, and touches upon them in his dedicatory Epistle in this way:

> We of the Church of England detest magical and superstitious healings; we have many poor parish ministers (of whom I myself am the least) who dare to handle and take up the argument and draw their pens and preach

<margin>against practices of this kind, etc.</margin>

Here you see the little plot of a precise, overly "pure," and far too meddlesome fellow <note>Fludd uses "precisi" (precise), a common jab at the Puritans of his day, whom he viewed as narrow-minded and hypocritical.</note> of the same stripe as Foster, I say, broken and set straight. And by Hercules, our Foster says rightly that the Church of England detests superstitious magical healings, as it is bound by law, and so I also do from the bottom of my heart. But I would certainly wish that he could give me a demonstrative proof that the power of the **Weapon-Salve** is magical or superstitious, and not truly natural. As for those arguments and proofs which he has produced so far to annihilate this kind of mystical healing, I find them (I testify before God and men) to be so weak and infirm that they have need of a draught of the strongest **water of life** <note>original: "aquæ vitæ," referring to strong distilled alcohol or brandy</note> lest they faint at the sight of truth. They are so thinly armed that even the slightest flick of truth would knock them down and deprive them of spirit. If he himself, with all his supports that his followers provide him, can prove it better, perhaps we shall be induced to believe it; but,

-> If the sky falls, we shall have larks. <- 
<note>This was a common proverb used to mock an impossibility or a ridiculous premise—essentially saying "anything is possible if you imagine the impossible."</note>

---

The second reason, or worldly intent, which he veils and covers with a mask of simplicity and holiness, is for the sake of pure and private craftiness—namely, to acquire favor and to perform a secret service for the **Surgeons**, from whose lineage he himself is descended. For when he perceived that these brothers of his were reacting poorly and indignantly to that constant good which this magnetic healing daily provides to many of God's weak and wounded creatures—and that this occurred not without great prejudice to their practice and profit—he thought it would be worthy and a meritorious service to them if he undertook the slandering and uprooting of such an "eyesore" <note>original: "mali... ocularis," literally an ocular evil or something that hurts the eyes to see.</note> redounding upon that small Commonwealth of theirs. And those men, no doubt perceiving him to be impudently confident by nature, and also of small means (as appears in his Epistle to the Reader, where he says he is "below envy" and has only a modest ecclesiastical income), and for that reason more reckless and desperate, and consequently more fit for exploding, railing, and provoking his betters with malignant speeches and taunts—for who would be so foolish as to waste their money in the **Star Chamber** <note>The Star Chamber was a high court known for punishing "libels" or slanderous writings; Fludd suggests Foster is too poor to be worth suing there.</note> against a pamphleteer or rather a common scold who possesses little or nothing?—to condemn precipitously and preposterously, without any modesty or conscience, things which have already been judged by God; they were greatly delighted to choose this man as a "lost soul" among those previously lost flocks. They chose him as one who would hastily attack and undergo such a task without modesty, judgment, or wit—namely, to strip this **Weapon-Salve** of its reputation among men, and to weaken it with false and scandalous imputations, simply because I maintain that it is only "naturally magical," and for that reason lawful and in no way diabolical. Now, that this is the case is made manifest by four very evident observations: the first of which is because this business touches chiefly upon the "allodium" <note>A legal term for absolute ownership of land; here used metaphorically for the surgeons' professional monopoly.</note> of the Surgeons. And therefore it is not unlikely that they are his "friends" whom he cites in his dedicatory Epistle, where he speaks in this manner:

> I would certainly rather suffer the loss of my own reputation than have men lose their health. My friends, at whose request I undertook this treatise, etc.

Here you see his religious veil; and again he detects his own hidden craftiness: he pretends zeal for the preservation and salvation of many, and then says he undertook it at the petition of certain friends, some of whom he marks in the margin with capital letters: **I. S.** and **E. C.** Now, as to who these may be explicitly, the title of his Latin Epistle expresses it: you will find them, no doubt, when you recognize them as proud and seditious men, of "profound capacity" to determine this doubt which we have in hand. Magnanimous "sworn men," I say, who give their Verdict upon such a profound Philosophical Mystery. May God preserve their well-mannered Authorities for my friends, he says: **John Scott** and **Edward Chaley**, etc.

<margin>
I. S.
E. C.
</margin>
