# EXPRESSION OF PART II.

...and so on: Therefore, this task concerns no one more, as I have said, than the **Surgeons**, and the following events will confirm that he considers them his close friends. The second observation is that this author of ours, who was opposed to us, was the son of a barber-surgeon. Hence that remarkable reverence he bears toward them, by awarding them the title of **Esquires** <note>original: "Armigerorum," a play on words suggesting both "shield-bearers" and those who handle surgical "arms" or tools</note>. The third point is made manifest by that extraordinary and unusual Latin Letter, in a certain English book which he dedicated to them. Finally, it is because some of them (as I have been informed) went with him to congratulate him and to procure for him a license to print his book—directed both against the **Weapon-Salve** <term>Weapon-Salve: A "magnetic" medicinal ointment (Unguentum armarium) believed to heal a wound if applied to the weapon that caused it rather than the body</term> and against myself by name. They did this when more modesty and restraint should have guided them, rather than so seriously persisting in and seeking a License for a scandalous Pamphlet, a part of which, I say, touches upon myself by name in a shameful and infamous manner, intended to pluck at and wound (as much as was in their power) my unblemished fame and reputation. This happened immediately after that immodest and distorted composer of his had been rebuked, repulsed, and rejected by two modest and learned men for those same uncivil and slanderous insolences.

I acknowledge myself much obliged to these patrons of my Antagonist, and all the more so to one or two of my colleagues; some of them hissed at and mocked me because of such insults, which he had expressed with a certain ostentation in his book against me, both before his license and similarly thereafter. But, just as I despise their slanders—being far too low to sink my reputation—so I pray to God that He might forgive their follies and cast down their malice. I do not accuse them all, but only some: For indeed I know there are some among them of a more learned, discreet, and modest disposition; these I beg to also forgive me if I speak justly and to the point, since I find myself so indignantly ensnared by some of their society, whether I cared for them or not. Therefore, with their permission having been asked, I shall proceed in my History in this manner.

The aforementioned noble Captain Stiles, along with **Sir Bevis Tellwell** (who obtained this ointment from that noble person and performed many admirable and desperate cures through it) and Mr. Wells of Deptford (a learned and honest gentleman), have cured at least—as they wish to prove—a thousand men by this method of healing. And now indeed there are many others, both men and women, who have made a profit from this **Weapon-Salve**, and daily perform much benefit in this kingdom through it. Hence the grief, hence the tears! <note>original: "Hinc dolor, hinc Lachrymæ!" a variation of a classical Latin idiom expressing the root of a problem</note> From this, I say, arises the grief and torment for the Surgeons, as much those of the city of London as those of any other condition. And do they not have cause enough, when they lose that quantity of practice which would have filled and furnished their purses?

<margin>Acts 19</margin> Was this not the case of the Ephesian silversmiths? They perceived that their gain, which they earned and produced in their calling and art through the idol of **Diana**, was likely to perish through the miraculous virtues of St. Paul, both in healing diseases and otherwise. (For in the same chapter it is said that God worked no small miracles by the hand of St. Paul, so that handkerchiefs were brought from his body to the sick, and diseases departed and Demons went out from them). In the health-giving preaching of Christ, they stirred up a tumult in the whole city at the persuasion and petition of a certain man named **Demetrius**, a silversmith. They did this so that in this way they might decree against and abolish such preachings and the miraculous operations of St. Paul, both in healing and otherwise, which he performed not only in that city but throughout all Asia, crying out against all truth and reason: *Great is Diana of the Ephesians!* Thus they wrongly inferred that the Doctrine of St. Paul was erroneous and his miracles were Diabolical. Does not this champion and Athlete of the Surgeons (I mean **Mr. Foster**) play the part of that Ephesian Demetrius in every point? He conspires with the craftsmen of his father's calling to stir up the whole city, and indeed the region itself, to murmur and grieve at the action of that virtuous gift which God has bestowed upon the **Weapon-Salve**. They cry out against those who use it for the glory of God and the <term>Well-being</term> <note>original: "Euexian" from the Greek "euexia," meaning good health or physical fitness</note> of their neighbor, because it detracts from and diminishes the profit and gain of the Surgeons' calling. Did not Demetrius, under the pretext of zeal and religion toward the false and Heathen Goddess Diana, detract as much as he could from the honor and Glory of Jesus Christ—and that chiefly for the sake of the profit and gain of the society of silversmiths? And does not Mr. Foster in a similar way, under a hypocritical shadow or a veil of sincerity, ascribe the power of healing through the **Weapon-Salve** to the Devil, the false God of this world? And by so doing, he detracts from and diminishes the Divine honor and power of the true God who made heaven and earth, so that he may thereby infect and poison the opinions of men endowed with virtue and good judgment, and distract them from the truth with his false persuasions, just as Demetrius tried to do with the honest Ephesians. For, insofar as he and his Surgical faction seem to cry out like another Demetrius: *Great is our **Aesculapius*** <note>The Greco-Roman god of medicine and healing</note> *or the God of the Surgeons, and his inventions of Balsams, Plasters, and Un—*

Fludd is currently addressing the accusation that healing a wound at a distance (by applying ointment to the weapon rather than the body) is a diabolical or magical act. He argues instead that such healing is a natural, divine process rooted in the "spirit of the blood."</meta>

# FOSTER’S SPONGES.

...of ointments: and consequently, they claim the Weapon-Salve <term>Weapon-Salve: A controversial "sympathetic" medicine, also known as the "ointment of the armory," which was applied to the weapon that caused a wound rather than to the patient’s body</term> is Diabolical because it thoroughly heals all wounds miraculously at a distance and not through contact. Did not Galen <note>Galen was a 2nd-century Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for over a thousand years; Fludd often contrasts Galen's "materialist" approach with his own "spiritual" chemistry</note> similarly rail against Christ and his disciples, because they healed at such a miraculous distance, and did so without any visual demonstration of how it worked? And why? The reason was because he could not cure spiritually at a distance, but only in a crude manner and by immediate physical contact.

Therefore, if in the manner of the Ephesian Market-Overseer <note>original: "Agoranomi Ephesij," referring to the official in Acts 19 who tried to calm the riot stirred up by Demetrius</note>, I seek and endeavor with words of reason and truth to appease the unfair, unjust rumors and excessive zeal stirred up by this sponge-bearing Demetrius <note>Fludd nicknames his opponent William Foster "Demetrius" after the silversmith in Acts 19 who opposed St. Paul to protect his trade; "sponge-bearing" refers to the title of Foster's book, *The Sponge*</note> of ours, and with words of truth ascribe that healing power to God the Father and His Son Jesus Christ, to whom it belongs—and if I openly renounce the devil and all his ministers for every good thing, and for that reason regard any health-giving healing as belonging to God the Creator of all, and certainly not to any lowly creature, much less to the devil—do you think I wander from the truth? Was it not, I ask, because of these very words of Paul, as in his sermon at Athens, that Demetrius and his craftsmen were so greatly moved and disturbed?

<margin>Acts 17:24.</margin>
The Lord, he said, who made heaven and earth, does not dwell in temples made by hands, nor is he worshiped by human hands as if he needed anything, since he gives life, breath, and all things to everyone: He created the entire human race from one blood, etc. For in him we live, move, and have our being; for we are also his offspring, etc. Therefore, insofar as we are the offspring of God, we ought not to think that the Deity is like gold and silver, or stone carved by human art and invention.

This Demetrius and his followers were indignant, specifically because the Deity is not like gold or silver carved by art; for if they had accepted this, no one would have bought the idols of Diana fashioned by them. In a similar way, this Surgical faction cries out against the Weapon-Salve, fearing that few wounded people will seek them for treatment, since what can be done through fewer means is done in vain through more. It would be pointless for a wounded man to be tortured by blows, biting corrosives, incisions, and the painful insertion of tents <term>tents: small plugs of lint or absorbent material inserted into a wound to keep it open or apply medication, often causing great pain</term>, besides the notable contracts and fees for cures—and perhaps even little oversight and care from the Surgeons—when an immediate Divine act brings it about for free, gently, without painful tents or heavy incisions, and does so honorably without any bad conscience. 

For indeed, the spirit belongs to God, which works equally in the blood and the ointment. For I beseech you, observe the words of St. Paul; if they moved the Ephesian goldsmiths, they will much more provoke such tenacious Surgeons, who intend to strangle and suffocate this excellent and Divine virtue in the Weapon-Salve, whose origin is blood. So that you may grasp these words with greater solidity, it is necessary to gather and collect these three things distinctly from them: First, that it is the Lord who created heaven and earth. From which we conclude that He who founded heaven and earth is the guide and operator in both spirit and body of both the greater world [the universe] and the lesser world [man]. And therefore, neither a witch nor the Devil can work for the health, sustenance, and preservation of either, as the following argument will confirm. 

For the Text says: *He gives life, breath, and all things.* Then He formed the entire human race from one blood and Spirit. And consequently, He works all things in all, and in human blood generally, both for life and for health. And again: The spirit of the bones of a dead man—and consequently their growth <note>Fludd is likely referring to "moss" or "usnea" grown on the skulls of the dead, a common ingredient in the Weapon-Salve</note>, which originally takes its rise from human blood, in which the Spirit of life lies hidden on God’s part—has a homogeneous relationship to the blood of a living man. For, as the Text says, the entire human race is from one blood only; and therefore this union of harmonic or sympathetic symphony is not easily limited by Mr. Foster's imaginary "sphere of activity." For the following Text to the Athenians is: *In him we live, move, and have our being.* And lastly, that we are the offspring of God, and for this reason Christ did not disdain to call us his brothers and children of God. 

Nor is it enough to say (as those precisionists <note>original: "precisi," likely referring to strict Puritans or dogmatic theologians</note> are accustomed to do) that this is understood only of believers and not of infidels. For St. Paul at that very time, when he was preaching this Doctrine, was speaking to Idolaters and others worshipping unknown Gods, and he simply wanted them to know that they live, move, and have their being in the true God, and that this Lord gives life, breath, and being, and that they are all of God's offspring. He taught all this truly, so that from there—
<margin>Romans 11:36.</margin>
—having dismissed and left behind the false Gods, they might turn to the true Lord and only God, from whom, through whom, and in whom they are and persist in their essence, as he declares elsewhere.

Therefore, let that Goddess Diana of Demetrius and Foster’s "curative Devil" fall from memory; let the devil be deprived of all imaginary power and practice in curing wounds. And finally, let the expectations of all greedy Surgeons be hissed off and annihilated by this inviolable assertion of the Apostle:

<margin>1 Corinthians 8:5.</margin>
Even if there are those who are called Gods either in heaven or on earth (as there are many Gods and Lords and rulers), yet for us there is one God, who is that Father, from whom are all things and we in him; and one Lord Jesus Chri-

He asserts that Surgeons reject the salve because its simple, painless, and free healing threatens their profitable and painful surgical methods.</summary>

<header>EXPLANATION OF PART II.</header>

...Christ <note>original: "stus," the end of the word Christus</note>, through whom are all things, and we through him: But not everyone possesses that knowledge. <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 8:6-7</note>

Let Mr. Foster <note>William Foster, Fludd's primary antagonist in this debate</note> note this well: It is as if he were saying: Although Demetrius of Ephesus <note>The silversmith in Acts 19 who opposed St. Paul to protect the cult of Diana</note> with his followers attributed all power to the false goddess Diana, and Foster attributes the secret and wonderful power of healing through the Weapon-Salve <term>Weapon-Salve: Also called "armory ointment," a preparation applied to the weapon that caused a wound to heal the patient through "sympathy"</term> to the Devil, as the Prince of this world, without any consideration of this Text: Nevertheless, it is certain that it is God the Father, from whom are all things; and consequently, this act of healing (and through our Lord Jesus Christ, through whom are all things) is a blessed Gift of healing. For Solomon asserts that it is the Word <note>original: "mundum," likely a typo for "Verbum" (the Word) or referring to the "spirit of the world" in a Neoplatonic sense</note> which heals all things: But, says the Text, "not everyone has that knowledge." Wherefore Mr. Foster is the more to be blamed, who by professing the name of a Minister of God, is ignorant on this point—or at the very least, if he did know it, he would fall into just reproach before both God and men, as one who speaks so obtusely and against his own conscience for the sake of some private society or worldly affection. Indeed, it easily appears that he was then troubled in his conscience, since he wrote so hesitantly and ill-advisedly concerning the original cause of this cure. 

For first of all, on page 8, he says it is not the ointment that heals, but the Devil, namely by the secret application of the remaining medicines. Elsewhere, specifically on page 17, he acknowledges that the act of healing is the Ointment, but concludes it is Magical by reason of the superstitious observations in the collection of ingredients and in the anointing of the weapons. Finally, on page 39, he seems to assert that it is the washing of the wound with urine and the diligent, careful attention in preserving its cleanliness that performs this cure. By these words, he strips this ointment of all virtue and actual power from the Devil. Yet on page 7, he says that it is God alone who heals at such a distance, insofar as His essence is infinite and He is all in all, and truly not any Angel. The good man, as you see, holds various opinions; may God call him back and render his "little brain" <note>original: "cerebellum"</note> more tranquil, of a more solid consistency and a more constant nature, otherwise we shall be troubled with many words in the future; but that is little to the matter. 

I hear that he threatens me with a volume of certain "impieties" which he has found in my works. Indeed, I hold this as certain and am persuaded that he will merely play the ape or parrot to that Mersenne and that Gassendi <note>Marin Mersenne and Pierre Gassendi, famous French philosophers and scientists who also criticized Fludd's mystical theories</note>; for he confesses those men to be his masters. Therefore, I shall provide and supply him with the same seasoning or response as quickly as I showed to them. Let him only appear, if he dares, even protected by whatever bodyguard his domestic followers can provide him; indeed, I care nothing for him, nor for Mersenne, nor for Lanovius, nor for Gassendi, his "popish" masters: For I have (as I hope) the shield of truth for my defense.

I shall now proceed to the chapter immediately following, in which I am to describe several accounts of certain Magical cures, which in my opinion are much more wonderful than these of the weapon-salve.

### CHAPTER VI.

->In this chapter several magnetic or sym-<-
->pathetic effects are described, which<-
->appear much more wonderful than those<-
->of the Weapon-Salve.<-

Sir Nicholas Gilbourne <note>A contemporary of Fludd and an English gentleman</note> related these words to me in his letter: Lately (he said), Lady Raleigh <note>original: "Domina Balegli," referring to the wife of Sir Walter Raleigh</note> was at Eastwell, at the home of the Countess of Winchelsea. When we fell into some talk regarding the Weapon-Salve, she told me that her recently deceased husband, Sir Walter Raleigh <note>The famous explorer and courtier to Queen Elizabeth I</note>, was accustomed to suddenly stop the hemorrhage of any human wound (even if he were far or a long distance from it) if he had a headband or a handkerchief, or some other small piece of linen dipped in the blood of the man sent to him. If this was done by the art of the Devil, I truly think that a man so wise as Sir Walter Raleigh would either have abandoned it, or at least would not have practiced that method of curing, or rather, of stopping the flow of blood.

There are four stories besides these which I am to relate to you, the first two of which are foreign; the latter two took place among our own countrymen, namely the English.

The first of the overseas stories happened in Italy, and was so famous and notable there that to this very day it is like a wellspring in the mouths of the inhabitants, as we have from the report of those who have traveled through those regions; indeed, there are even authors of good reputation who have committed it to the record of their writings. The matter was as follows: There was a certain highly noble Italian of the Great Lords, who by chance lost his nose in a battle or skirmish. This Nobleman was advised by his physicians that, having taken one of his slaves, he should inflict a wound in the slave's arm and immediately join his own wounded nose to the wounded arm of the slave, and bind it there firmly for a time...

<sig>E 2</sig> <meta>catchword: while (dum)</meta>

<summary>Fludd refutes William Foster's inconsistent arguments against the Weapon-Salve, labeling Foster's views as contradictory and dependent on French critics like Mersenne. He introduces historical accounts of "sympathetic" healing, including a blood-stanching technique used by Sir Walter Raleigh and a famous Italian case of nasal reconstruction using a servant's arm.</summary>

<keywords>Robert Fludd, William Foster, Weapon-Salve, Sir Walter Raleigh, sympathetic magic, magnetic healing, Marin Mersenne, Pierre Gassendi, rhinoplasty, blood-stanching</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s defense of "magnetic" or sympathetic healing against the attacks of William Foster. Fludd is using case studies of sympathetic transplantation and "transplantation of disease" to trees to prove that a universal spirit connects all living things, even across vast distances.</meta>

<header>FOSTER’S SPONGES.</header>

...until the flesh of the one was united and assimilated to the other. That nobleman obtained the consent of one of his slaves <note>The Latin *mancipium* refers to a legal bondservant or slave.</note> through a generous promise of freedom and reward: the doubled flesh was united and made into the same and a similar substance, and a portion of flesh was cut out from the slave’s arm and shaped into a nose for that nobleman. It was then treated and fitted by surgeons so that it resembled a true and genuine nose. After the cure and his payment, the slave was manumitted <note>formally set free from slavery</note> and departed for Naples. It happened, however, that the slave fell ill and died; at that very moment, the nose of the aforementioned nobleman began to rot, having caught a gangrene. Thereafter, the part or portion of the nose which he possessed from the dead man was cut away on the advice of the Doctors. Then, with a cheerful spirit based on the previous experience, he followed the advice of the same physician: that is, that he should wound his own arm in a similar manner and apply the wound to his wounded and mutilated nose, and patiently endure until all was completed as before. He underwent the danger with courage and patience, and thus the nose remained with him until his death.

What then? Should we consider this work the act of the Spirit of God in man, or a deceptive and illusory operation and imposture of the Devil? By Hercules, it will necessarily be judged by wise men to proceed from that benign act of the life-giving Spirit of God, which was working through the way of life and growth in both. Therefore, it is no wonder (with one man living in Bologna in Italy and the other residing in Naples) that, according to Mr. Foster’s dogma, neither the high and lofty mountains of Tuscany <note>original: "Hetruriæ"</note>, nor even the highest Apennine mountains could stop the meeting and movement of these two spirits—or rather, of one spirit extended and continued in two bodies, like a cord stretched from two ends over such a long distance. Certainly, Mr. Foster will say that this is Magical and Diabolical.

### The second overseas History was this.

When I was staying in Rome, I entered into friendship with a very learned and ingenious man named Mr. Grater; he was Swiss by nation, and because of his remarkable skill in Mathematics and the art of motion and the invention of Machines, he was held in great esteem by Cardinal St. George. This Gentleman taught me the best of my craft in such practices: and among other things he handed down to me this Magnetic experiment as a great secret, assuring me that it had been proven in his own country upon many people with happy success. "When," he said, "someone has a limb that is thinned and slender, or consumed in another way—such as an arm dried up by a wasting disease <note>original: "marasmo"</note>, a leg, a foot, or the like, which physicians call Atrophy of the loins <term>Atrophy of the loins: A historical medical term for a wasting or withering of the lower body or extremities.</term>—you must cut from that limb (whether it be a foot or an arm) the nails, the hair, or a portion of the skin. Then, having perforated a willow tree with an auger down to the marrow of the tree, you shall put the clippings of nails and skin into the hole and stop it up with a peg made of the same wood. Always observing that in this action the moon is increasing in light and the good planets are in a multiplicative sign, such as Gemini, and are fortunate and powerful over Saturn, who is the great dryer. You will find the same effect," he said, "if you enclose the nails and hair cut from the limb into the root of a Hazel tree <note>original: "Corgli," likely a misspelling of "Coryli" (hazel).</note>; let the hole be properly closed with the bark of the tree and then covered with earth; and," said he, "it is proven by experience that as the tree daily grows and flourishes, so also the patient will gradually recover health. But you must diligently observe the motion of the celestial bodies, and especially of the Sun and Moon, when this is performed." And for this purpose, he revealed to me the convenient time when, surely, the preparation for such a cure ought to be made. But alas! What have I done? It is enough now for Mr. Foster to cry out that this is Magical.

Behold here superstition in the highest degree! Did he not say on page 7 that it is Astrological, and for that reason a superstitious observation, to gather any ingredient or perform anything by attending to and waiting for when the Moon should be in such or such a celestial house? And he says that from the scriptures, Astrologers, Evil Magicians <term>Cacimagos: From the Greek 'kakos' (evil) and 'magos' (magician); a term used to distinguish black magic from 'natural magic'.</term>, and sorcerers are joined together like birds of one and the same feather and bound into a single bundle.

A judgment truly fulfilled in this "gentleman" of the city! His blindness leads him to this, as in other things: For, first, he concludes that all Magic is generally to be condemned and is Diabolical, because one species—or rather a limb of it—is deservedly to be banished from the memory of Christians. As if there were no Natural Magic <term>Natural Magic: The study of the hidden powers of nature (like magnetism or herbs) which was considered a legitimate science by many Renaissance scholars.</term> through which Solomon knew all the mysteries in nature and their operations; indeed, as if the three Sabeans <note>The Magi or Three Wise Men who visited the infant Jesus.</note> had made known and revealed that the true King of the Jews was born through Diabolical Magic.

What now, Mr. Foster? Were these three wise men Evil Magicians, or were they such as the Scripture approves, and we Christians celebrate a feast in their memory?

<summary>Fludd recounts two "magnetic" healing stories: a successful nose transplant that responded sympathetically to the donor's death, and a cure for limb wasting using tree transplantation and astrology. He argues these are examples of "Natural Magic" rather than the "Diabolical Magic" Foster claims.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s defense of "magnetic" or sympathetic medicine. Having established the reality of sympathetic action in the previous section, he now turns to defend the theoretical framework of his work—specifically the roles of Magic and Astrology—against his critic William Foster.</meta>

<page-num>Fol. 19.</page-num>
# EXPRESSION OF THE SECOND PART

->A true ape of the monk Mersenne!<- <note>Marin Mersenne was a French scholar and critic of Fludd; Fludd implies Foster is merely mimicking Mersenne's earlier attacks.</note> For he condemns all Magic without any exception of its types, and thus fails to remember that in the Persian language, a **Magus** is a wise man or a priest. In a similar manner, this "good man," following the habit of his master, condemns and rejects all **Astrology** because of that part of it which is truly superstitious and illicit. He does not consider that the truth in both—in Magic as well as Astrology—has been falsely contaminated and snatched away for abuse by worldly superstitions. From this cause it has proceeded that the good things are loathed and condemned in the eyes of the unskilled, along with the bad, for the sake of the bad; in this way, goodness is swallowed up by worthless men through darkness, without any distinction at all.

Therefore, I would like our "sponge-bearing" antagonist <note>A reference to the title of William Foster’s book, *The Hoplocrisma-Spongus* (The Sponge to Wipe Away the Weapon-Salve).</note> to know that there are four general types of **Astrology**. The **first** deals with the changing of the air and the prediction of storms, diseases, the price of grain, or fertility, etc. The **second** predicts the alterations and changes of states, as well as wars or the peaceful disposition in the minds of men. The **third** interprets the **elections of times** <note>The practice of choosing the most auspicious moment to begin an action.</note> and of births. The **last** is directed toward the fabrication of **Characters, seals, and images**, which—because it mixes itself with superstitious actions and has been turned into an instrument for the abuse and scandal of impious men, and especially because a **Diabolical insinuation** toward vice and impiety can easily be detected in it by all true Christians—must be repudiated and rejected as illicit. 

What then? Is the science of the Calendar-writers to be abolished because of these exceptions of our Foster? Shall physicians be compelled to abandon and neglect the hours of their **Election** in gathering simples <note>Medicinal herbs.</note>, or in bloodletting, or in cutting hair and nails, or in stopping fluxes, or in making the belly fluid, because of the discouragement of such a "great" man? Does not the "friend of physicians" assert that the influence of heaven can assist the operation of medicine? For (he says himself) laxative medicines are often administered by unskilled doctors under a heavenly influence that works the contrary or "styptic" <note>An effect that contracts tissues or stops bleeding, the opposite of a laxative.</note> effect, and thus they are hindered. Similarly, he says: whenever medicine is given to stop a flux while the influence of heaven is slippery and laxative, the medicine then loses its effect. For this reason, **Haly** <note>Haly Abenragel, an 11th-century Arab astrologer and physician.</note> reports that a physician unskilled in Astrology is like a blind man exploring the way with a stick. And **Ptolemy** says a good astrologer can avert many future effects of the stars. Do not **Galen** and **Hippocrates** speak more than enough in their critical treatises regarding the necessity of observing the motion of the Moon? But let me pass over these.

What shall we say to the farmer's observations of times, both in sowing and in reaping? If this is not enough to halt and restrain the violence of our adversary, we shall come to the testimonies of **Holy Scripture** for the confirmation of the selection of times, as well as to prove that the influences of heaven produce both benign and malignant effects.

<margin>Eccles. 3.</margin>
> As to the first, it is said: There is a time for planting, and a time for uprooting that which was planted; there is a time for war, and a time for peace, etc.

<margin>Eccles. 7.</margin>
> And the son of Sirach says: In a good day, enjoy that which is good, and beware of an evil day; as God created the one, so has He ordained the other. <note>Fludd cites "Syracides," referring to the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus), though he marks it as Ecclesiastes in the margin.</note>

<margin>Ephes. 6.</margin>
And **St. Paul** wishes us to be clothed with the shield of God, so that we may be able to resist the Devil in the **unfortunate day**. Now, therefore, if the stars are the dividers and guides of times, as **Moses** tells us, surely their influence—whether it be good or evil—causes the good or evil Angels to have more or less dominion over creatures. That there are evil influences from above is testified by these words of **David**:

<margin>Psalm. 121.</margin>
> God is your keeper; the Sun shall not strike you by day, nor the Moon by night.

Is it not well known among the common people that the Moon is the cause of **Epilepsy**? Where, in the clearest words in the Gospel, it is said that the possessed man was "lunatic" <note>original: *lunaticum*; literally "moon-struck."</note>?

<margin>Job 38:31</margin>
Similarly, that there are good influences from above is argued by this text from **Job**:
> Can you hold back the pleasant influence of the **Pleiades**, or loose the bands of **Orion**? Can you bring forth **Mazzaroth** <note>The twelve signs of the Zodiac.</note> in its season? Can you also guide **Arcturus** with his sons? Do you know the course of heaven? Or can you set its rule upon the earth? etc.

In this text, we find mention made of the influence of beneficial stars; and here it is also expressly noted that the heavens have their powers over the earth.

Therefore, I boldly affirm that all Astrology is neither interdicted nor forbidden, insofar as a special observation can be held by wise men concerning the influence of the Stars. To this end, the **hours of elections** are to be observed in a due manner, according to this or that influence which is most convenient and proper for our work. Furthermore, since Mr. Foster seems to despise the **12 signs of the Zodiac** and their essential operations upon the earth, let him see that those who have made marginal notes upon the Text interpret the word or term **Mazzaroth** to signify the 12 signs which possess the 12 celestial houses; which being granted, observe the conclusion of the Text: *Can you apply its rule to the earth?* From which it is clear that the 12 signs have a special rule over the earth and the creatures upon it, and that by the destination and ordination of God.

He distinguishes between "wise" astrology used by physicians and "superstitious" astrology used for diabolical purposes.</summary>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s point-by-point rebuttal of William Foster. Having just discussed the four types of Astrology, Fludd now argues that choosing specific times for medical preparation is a natural science, not "Cacomagic" (evil magic), and provides folk and "domestic" examples of sympathetic healing to prove his point.</meta>

# FOSTER’S SPONGES
<margin>1</margin>

From this it is made manifest that there is no **evil magic** <note>original: "Cacomagicam," from the Greek 'kakos' (evil) and 'magia' (magic).</note> in the observation of times, days, or hours in which this or that star holds dominion for the gathering of ingredients, or for the preparation and adaptation of medicines or other things suited to human healing, as Mr. Foster wrongly asserts.

In the conclusion of this section, one may discern through the previously mentioned experiment how the vegetative force of a plant works upon the waste parts of a certain wasted or withered limb, no differently than the ointment works upon blood that has been shed. One can see how the spirit of those **nails**, the skull, and the skin <note>Fludd refers here to common ingredients in sympathetic medicine, believed to retain the "vital spirit" of the person they came from.</note> participates with the spirit of the thin and weakened limb. This is no different than the spirit in the blood of the **Ointment** <note>The "Weapon-Salve" applied to the weapon rather than the wound.</note> communicating with the bloody spirits of the wounded man, or as the waste of urine communicates with blood infected by **jaundice** <note>original: "icteritia."</note>, as will be told to you hereafter. Otherwise, they could not convey or breathe out the nourishing spirit of the plant to the affected limb, nor could the spirits in the failing or magnetically affected limb attract the nourishing spirit of the plant to themselves, so that by the addition of its power, it might prosper and recover all the more quickly.

It is commonly observed among us—indeed, it is a familiar practice in **old wives' remedies**—that if warts, whether on the hand or some other limb, are thoroughly rubbed with a piece of fresh beef and that beef is then buried in the ground, the warts gradually decrease as the beef rots in the earth. This happens even if the person infected with the warts lives far away from the place where the beef was buried and committed to the earth. Is it necessary that this kind of healing also be deemed **evil magic** or diabolical? Indeed, by Hercules, it must be so just like the rest, if what Mr. Foster and his followers assert is true.

I could indeed hand over to the memory of any reader those accustomed conclusions in **natural magic** which, if properly weighed, would in my opinion manifest more than enough to any wise judgment how far removed they are from any diabolical practice or commerce. But, because I fear that by doing so I might be more tedious than delightful to the curious, I come briefly to two domestic stories which I promised you before.

The first of our domestic stories is this: at this present time, a noble, honest, and religious lady lives in London who takes the herb called **Sundew** <note>original: "rosa Solis" (Drosera). A carnivorous plant whose leaves move and trap moisture.</note> (which has small pods all around it that can open, dilate, and contract again) and places it into **plantain water**, and the plant closes and contracts. Therefore, when a woman is laboring in childbirth, this noblewoman gives her a little of the plantain water to drink. Even if the woman in labor seems, according to the readiest of midwives, about to give birth, if the said noblewoman perceives the vegetable has contracted, she concludes the midwife is deceived and the matter is not yet ready—and indeed the outcome proves her right. This story was related and confirmed by an honorable Doctor and his Apothecary, who assert that certain midwives at this time put this natural conclusion into practice.

Now, I am certainly not ignorant that Mr. Foster will say this too is diabolical and superstitious. Good God! What will this man leave to be ascribed and attributed to the **Sun** as the actor in all operations, both common and mystic? For there is nothing hidden or rare in this world which this "superior" actor of the devil (as if I were his "inferior" sorcerer, as he calls me) does not attribute to his master the Devil. It is as if God is only to be acknowledged as acting in common and visible things, but regarding all hidden mysteries, their activity must necessarily proceed from the devil and be attributed to him alone. Finally, we perceive a certain admirable **sympathy** between the vegetable, mineral, and animal kingdoms and the parts of the human body: for instance, **Coltsfoot** <note>original: "Forfara."</note>, formed in the likeness of lungs, is beneficial to the lung; **Rupturewort** <note>original: "Herniaria."</note> for a rupture; **Liverwort** <note>original: "Hepatica."</note> for the liver; **Eyebright** <note>original: "Euphrasia."</note> for the eyes. And again among minerals: **gold** for the heart, **silver** for the brain, **sulfur** for the lungs, and **iron** for the spleen if it is carried near the diaphragm or the region of the spleen. And why should an herb not have the same relation and correspondence in nature with the womb, by reason of the plantain water which communicates the nature of the one with the other, with the air acting as the common medium?

The second domestic story is this: there is a nobleman in this kingdom, of no mean or common family, title, or right among the English Nobility—a most wise, grave, elderly, and religious gentleman, I say—who in his time has freed over a hundred people from **jaundice** <note>original: "aurigine."</note> while the patient was distant or absent by 10, 20, 30, 40—indeed, as he and others have reported, nearly 100 miles from him. Many of those he thus cured had struggled long under the heavy burden of this disease before they came to him, so that the administration of common physicians' receipts could scarcely overcome the illness. He performed this through his servants at home and shared the secret with certain friends living abroad, among whom he was pleased to place me. The patient's **urine** is therefore sent to this Nobleman. His method of curing is this: he takes ashes from woods commonly known and growing here among us in England; he makes a **paste** from this wood-ash with the urine, reserving a little urine for another purpose. This mass, thus worked and prepared with the urine, is divided into 7 or 9 balls or **pills** <note>original: "magdaleones seu bolos."</note>; he adds to them a little of the remaining urine and applies a thread of **saffron** to those portions of urine. And so, without further trouble, he places the balls or boluses in some secret place where they cannot be disturbed, lest the cure be hindered. And experience has taught the world that very many jaundiced or "rainbow-colored" people infected with the "gold-disease" <note>original: "ictericos vel arquatos & aurigine infectos." All three terms refer to the yellow or variegated skin of jaundice.</note> have been thoroughly healed by these simple means.

<summary>Robert Fludd defends natural astrology and sympathetic medicine by providing examples of "natural magic," such as using Sundew to predict childbirth and curing jaundice from a distance using a patient's urine mixed with ash.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, sympathy, natural magic, sundew, jaundice, signatures, William Foster, weapon-salve, plantain, folk medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s argument regarding "sympathetic" cures. He is explaining how a common folk remedy for jaundice—which involves mixing a patient's urine with ashes and saffron—is a natural process of "spiritual" communication rather than the demonic witchcraft that his opponent, William Foster, claims it to be.</meta>

# THE EXPRESSION OF THE SECOND MEMBER

...and this is known to thousands—good God! How diabolical this medicine will seem to the pure and chaste eyes of Mr. Foster. He will instantly exclaim: "This is abominable and diabolical witchcraft, and those who use it are sorcerers and conjurers!" But cease your railing and slandering of this man; for he is of such a character that he does not "bear coals" <note>original: "carbones non perfert," a Latin idiom meaning he will not endure insults or being "burned" by slander without a fight.</note>, but will punish any of your immodest insults. What? I ask: Does the devil hide in the sick man's urine, or is he present in the burnt ashes of the wood? (They say that burning the belongings of witches makes all magical power vanish.) Or is he perhaps in the strands of Saffron that are dipped into the urine? Not at all; he is instead in the minds of those who dream such things. For it is certain that the plant and the saffron possess hidden properties for curing **jaundice** <note>original: "icteritiam."</note>.

The entire mystery of this mode of healing, therefore, regards the relationship between the blood of the infected person and the serum (the watery part) of the blood, which carries with it a part of the natural "Sun" and the "tincture" of the blood, and a part of the remains of the jaundiced humor, which makes the urine so yellow in color. Therefore, the **spirit of the blood** <note>In Fludd's philosophy, the "vital spirit" resides in the blood and can maintain a connection to its source even outside the body.</note>, hiding with its active force in the tincture and the salt found in that watery excrement, is buried in that medicinal earth or ashes (in which the "Sun of the plant" resides) and is stirred up by the continuity it maintains with that spirit of life which still lives in the veins of the infected person. 

Consequently, when the urine is soaked into and mixed with that medicinal earth and tempered with saffron, the life-giving spirit of the patient—striving for the preservation of life—assists its "like" in the urine. It likewise awakens and revivifies the spirit in the salt of the ashes to act and struggle against the jaundiced humor in the urine. When that humor yields and is gradually overcome, it causes the same humor in the affected body to die off and vanish. Furthermore, the spirit hiding in the salt, awakened partly by the stirred-up spirit of the plant and partly by the emanating spirit of the patient, sends back or leaves behind a curative property throughout the entire channel of the blood.

I leave this to the more serious consideration of the learned, who know better how to judge the hidden and abstruse operations of the incorruptible Spirit of God, concluding all things with these words of the Apostle: <margin>1 Cor. 12</margin> "God works all things in <margin>Rom. 1</margin> all"; and "from Him, through Him, and in Him are <margin>1 Tim. 6</margin> all things"; and finally: <margin>1 Cor. 4</margin> "There are those called Gods in heaven and on earth; but to us there is one Father, from whom are all things and we in Him, and one Lord Jesus Christ, through whom are all things and we through Him." <note>Fludd is weaving together several New Testament verses—primarily from 1 Corinthians and Romans—to argue that all natural healing comes from God's omnipresent power, not the Devil.</note> Therefore, it is vainly and foolishly said by Mr. Foster that the cure of the <term>Ointment of the Armory</term> <note>The "Weapon-Salve," which healed wounds by being applied to the weapon that caused them.</note> is performed by the devil, the enemy of Jesus Christ, and not by Christ Himself. For Jesus is the only Savior, Healer, and Curer of both soul and body; as He holds all powers and authorities under His dominion, He is accustomed to incite His good Angels to work goodness, and not the malignant Angels, whom He had ordained for a completely contrary office. I will not say that this assertion of his is a form of blasphemy, but it is no better: at the very least, it is a notable kind of idolatry to ascribe the good works of God to the essential act of the worst and most wicked of all His creatures, whom God appointed for an entirely different use—namely, to be His scourging and destroying minister or Angel.

Now I shall proceed to the particular defense of my own doctrine, expressed in my <term>mystical anatomy</term> <note>original: "mystica mea anatomia." Fludd is likely referring to his work *Anatomiae Amphitheatrum*, where he describes the spiritual/mystical structures of the human body.</note>, against which Mr. Foster so bitterly inveighs. He crows with such confidence, like a cock on his own dungheap, before he even has occasion to do so, gloriously claiming the palm for himself and proclaiming a trophy of his own praise before he has won the victory. The outcome proves the deeds; for truth does not need to be propped up by the fringes and trappings of words. It would have been safer for Mr. Foster if he had heard me speak first, before publicly gnawing at me with a **vicious tooth** <note>original: "dente Theodino," a classical reference to a sharp or biting critic.</note> and posting the titles of his book on my door to my infamy. Whether he acted discreetly or not, I leave to the free judgment of the world; and so I pass on to the final member of this treatise.

# FOSTER'S SPONGE. THE THIRD PART.

->In which the Author annihilates all those arguments and objections,<-
->which Mr. Foster produced with such invincible confidence,<-
->to refute his opinion expressed in his<-
->Mystical Anatomy, where he proves the action of<-
->curing by means of the weapon-salve to be<-
->purely natural and in no way<-
->Magical and diabolical.<-

### PRELUDE TO THIS PART.

->*In which the author expresses that his adversary's insults are founded upon mal-*<-
->*ice, and not truly upon any legitimate or due merit of his own: Here*<-
->*he also shows the method of his progression in this*<-
->*part along with the reasoning for the same.*<-

Any reader endowed with judgment can discern from Mr. Foster's scandalous vehemence against me regarding the composition of the subject of this part in my *Mystical Anatomy* <note>original: "Anatomia mea mystica." This was one of Fludd's major works where he integrated medicine, alchemy, and theology.</note>; it arises more from malice and spite against me, than from any sin or merit of mine, or any scandal committed by me either against him (insofar as I do not know him) or against any other. For without doubt, it was not for a slight occasion—such as that brief chapter was expressed in the aforementioned place—that he would have provoked me with such insults by the title of <term>Cacomagus</term> <note>From the Greek "kakos" (evil) and "magus" (magician). A term for a practitioner of "black" or demonic magic.</note>, as he did. He alleges the authority of his "prudent master," the monk [Marin] Mersenne, as an author as deep in taunts and vain insults as he is. 

But why, I ask, should he beat me with Mersenne's scandalous words in this writing of his, when he sees that that Monk is so thoroughly cut up by taunts and insults by his own friend and champion [Pierre] Gassendi? <note>Fludd is referencing a famous 17th-century intellectual feud. Mersenne and Gassendi were major French philosophers; while they were Fludd's rivals, Fludd gleefully points out their internal disagreements.</note> In his response, which he makes with Gassendi and [Jean de] Launoy against me, after I had completely entangled and snared him because of his follies, he would not even dare to open his mouth against me or repeat any word regarding the aforementioned insults of Magic, which in his book on Genesis, without any foundation, he entrusts to my charge. But does he fix all his envy and malice in this response upon certain "impieties" (as he entitles them) which he quite weakly hands over to my care and province? 

Again, since our domestic antagonist [Foster] says that I excused myself from Magic in the book titled *The Contest of Wisdom with Folly* <note>original: "Sophiæ cum Moria certamen," published by Fludd in 1629.</note>, and that Launoy says [this], the contrary of which is true; I must say to him that it is less fitting for a man of his profession to utter such a falsehood. For Launoy (though only in his own malicious way) clears me of that crime, alleging that my lack of skill or insufficiency in such matters was the reason he thought the opposite. And therefore I must say this to my English slanderer: that there is a <term>Star Chamber</term> <note>original: "Cameram esse Stellatam." The Star Chamber was a high court in England at the time, notorious for its power to punish libel and "infamies." Fludd is effectively threatening Foster with a lawsuit.</note> for punishing and fining such infamies, and consequently he will perhaps hear from me sooner than he expects, unless he tames and restrains his slanderous tongue better in the future. 

It is an argument savoring little of Philosophy and even less of Theology to insult and scandalize beyond measure without modesty: for Philosophy is the love of wisdom, and the wise man says that it is the part of fools to insult. Conversely: all Theology is founded upon love and charity; and Christ’s chief sermon was to love our brothers and cherish our neighbors as ourselves, and to exhort us not to judge our brothers prematurely. But to the matter at hand: whether this chapter of ours (judicious Reader) in which our censor makes such a strict inquiry, and against which he has formed a refutation so punctual...

<meta>catchword: ctualem,</meta>

<summary>Fludd begins the third section of his defense, accusing William Foster of malicious slander for labeling him an "evil magician." Fludd asserts that his "weapon-salve" is based on natural philosophy rather than sorcery and threatens Foster with legal action in the Star Chamber.</summary>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s defense against William Foster. Fludd is arguing that his theories on the "weapon-salve" are based on natural philosophy and "magnetic" sympathy rather than the "black magic" (cacomagia) Foster accuses him of.</meta>

Fol. 21.
EXPRESSION OF THE THIRD PART.

actual, does it contain within itself any **Evil-Magical** <note>original: "Cacomagicum"</note> work, on account of which I should be so sharply censured by our censor of precipitous wit, even at its very threshold? Do you discern in it anything that could incite our censor to make and undertake such a scandalous and less than Christian entry into the investigation of it, so that from there the unrefined and lay class of men might consider me a sorcerer or an **Evil-Magician**? As for men of a sharper nose <note>A Latin idiom for people of keen discernment or wit.</note>, I am certain and persuaded that they smile. But is this not an argument of envy, founded upon no solid foundation? And is he himself not worthy of the commiseration of any good Christian, as much for his immoderate envy as for several other things? For what does he possess in himself that could merit envy, since in his "Letter to the Reader" he confesses himself to be "below envy"? Indeed, as for me, I must agree with the opinion of the whole world and say that I would rather bear envy than pity. But, to return to our subject. The subject of this chapter, cited by me in my **mystical anatomy** <note>original: "anatomia mea mystica." This refers to Fludd’s major work where he explains the human body through a lens of alchemy and divine harmony.</note>, is solely a discourse on the natural and magnetic or attractive and sympathetic relationship or comparison observed to exist between two distinct substances of like nature, but differing in the distance of their location, such as between the **Magnet** and iron, or between blood and its salt of the same nature, in which the common vegetating spirit remains and resides secretly. And it is necessary that you also note here (honest reader) that in this particular book of my **mystical anatomy**, I have thoroughly treated the secret and occult properties of the spiritual or internal blood within the external, citing at the same time, according to the capacity of my wit, the harmonic effects which it works both through contact or immediate touch, as well as at a distance. Truly, I would wish to know where and in what I have offended by so doing, or how I have deserved Mr. Foster's offensive entry into the examination of this business; or whether, in my natural discourse on this subject, I bring into the open any **Diabolical** deceptions, circulations, witchcraft, or forbidden and illicit Characters and the like? But if you find nothing that pertains to any such **Diabolical Magic**, deliver your judgment as to whether such a prelude to this business was honest, decent, or in any way pertinent to the matter in hand. As for the use of this **Weapon Salve** <term>Unguentum armarium</term> <note>A controversial 17th-century medical treatment where an ointment was applied to the weapon that caused a wound, rather than the wound itself, supposedly healing the patient through "sympathy."</note>, I protest before God and man that I have never yet practiced it unto this day; but in my conscience, and by reason of a stricter inquiry than I have applied for any other cause to verify it, I find it so far free from any Diabolical superstition (which, God is my witness, I have always hated, as I also hate the Devil and all his works). I have heard so much of its virtuous operation that in the future—in spite of the envy of inhuman writers and babblers against it and its practice—I intend to use it and defend its lawful manner. For I am now more certain than ever before that it is a blessed power of God, and no act of the Devil, since it works for the health and relief of God's afflicted creatures.

->But, to return to our subject.<-

I shall finish the matter in three words (as they say), for I have already been rather long-winded in my preceding discourse. But before I make a beginning, I ask you to observe, benevolent reader, that our **sponge-bearer** <note>Fludd’s nickname for his opponent William Foster, whose book was titled *The Sponge* (Hoplocrisma-Spongus) because it aimed to "wipe away" Fludd's theories.</note> is quite halting and imperfect in the interpretation of my Text, stretching it far from its genuine and true nature so that he may retain his own sense rather than faithfully express my intention (as is proper, by Hercules!). Therefore, in the first place, I will set out for you in naked words the full and exact scope of my Latin Text, which I call my assertion, and then afterwards, in the next place, I will explain its exposition or collection. FOSTER’S SPONGES.
<note>The title "Spongiæ Fosterianæ" refers to Fludd "wiping away" Foster's arguments as if with a sponge.</note>

# THE THIRD PART.

->In which the Author annihilates all those arguments and objections which Mr. Foster produced with such invincible confidence to refute his opinion expressed in his **Mystical Anatomy** <note>original: "anatomia ejus mystica"</note>, where he proves the action of curing by the **weapon-salve** <note>term: "unguentum armarium" — a controversial 17th-century remedy applied to the weapon that caused a wound rather than the wound itself</note> is purely natural and by no means Magical or diabolical.<-

## PRELUDE TO THIS PART.

->*In which the author expresses that the insults of his adversary are founded upon malice, and not indeed upon any legitimate or due merit of his own: Here he also shows the method of his progress in this part, along with its rationale.*<-

Any reader endowed with judgment can discern from Mr. Foster's scandalous vehemence against me regarding the composition of the subject of this part in my **Mystical Anatomy**; it arises more from malice and spite against me than from any crime or merit of mine, or any scandal committed by me either against him (insofar as I do not know him) or against any other. For, without a doubt, he would not have provoked me with insults by the title of **Evil Magician** <note>original: "Cacomagi"</note> on such a slight occasion as that short chapter expressed in the aforementioned place, as he has done. He also cites the authority of his learned master, the Monk Mersenne <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French mathematician and philosopher who frequently attacked Fludd’s mystical views</note>, on this matter—an author as deep in mockery and vain insults as he is himself. But why, I ask, should he hammer Mersenne's scandalous words against me into his own writing, when he see that this very Monk is so thoroughly torn apart with mockery and insults by his own friend and champion Gassendi? <note>Pierre Gassendi, a contemporary philosopher who debated both Fludd and Mersenne</note>. In his response, which he made with Gassendi and Lanovius against me, after I had entirely entangled and snared him because of his follies, he did not dare to even open his mouth against me or repeat a single word. This concerns the aforementioned insults of Magic which, in his book on Genesis, he commits to my charge without any foundation. But does he fix all his envy and malice in this response upon certain impieties (as he titles them) which he very weakly delivers to my charge and province? 

Again, since our domestic antagonist says that I excused myself from Magic in the book titled: **The Conflict of Wisdom with Folly** <note>original: "Sophiæ cum Moria certamen"</note>, and that Lanovius says the contrary is true, I must tell him that it is unbecoming for a man of his profession to put forward such a falsehood. For Lanovius (though now malicious to the best of his ability) clears me of that crime, alleging that my lack of skill or insufficiency in such matters was the reason he thought the opposite. And therefore, I must say to my English slanderer that there is a **Star Chamber** <note>The "Camera Stellata," a high court in England used to prosecute libel and challenges to the social order</note> to punish and fine such infamies, and consequently he will perhaps hear from me sooner than he expects, unless he tames and restrains his slanderous tongue better in the future. It is an argument savoring of little Philosophy and less Theology to insult and scandalize beyond measure without modesty: for Philosophy is the love of wisdom, and the wise man says it is the part of a fool to insult. Conversely: all Theology is founded upon love and charity; and Christ’s primary sermon was to love our brothers and cherish our neighbors as ourselves, and to exhort us not to judge our brothers too hastily. But, to the point: regarding this chapter of ours (judicious Reader) in which our censor makes such a strict inquiry, and against which he has formed such a point-by-point refutation,

EXPRESSION OF MEMBER III. Fol. 21.

...point-by-point <note>original: "actualem"</note>, does it contain in itself any **Evil-Magical** <note>original: "Cacomagicum"</note> work, on account of which I should be so sharply censured by our censor—a man of headstrong character—even at its very threshold? Do you discern in it anything that could incite our censor to make and undertake such a scandalous and less than Christian entry into the investigation of it, so that the unrefined and lay sort of men might think me a **sorcerer** <note>original: "veneficum"</note> or an **Evil Magician**? As for men of a more refined judgment <note>original: "emunctioris naris homines," literally "men of a cleaner nose," an idiom for those with keen discernment</note>, I am certain and persuaded that they smile at this. But is this not a proof of envy, built upon no solid foundation? And is he not himself, as much for his immoderate envy as for several other things, worthy of the pity of any good Christian? For what does he possess in himself that could deserve envy, since in his "Letter to the Reader" he confesses himself to be "below envy"? Indeed, as for me, I must agree with the opinion of the whole world and say that I would rather bear envy than pity. 

But, to return to our matter. The subject of this chapter cited by me in my **Mystical Anatomy** <note>original: "anatomia mea mystica"</note> is solely a discourse on the natural and magnetic—or attractive and sympathetic—relation, or comparison, which is observed to exist between two distinct substances of a similar nature, but differing in the distance of their location; as between the **Magnet** and iron, or between blood and its salt of the same nature, in which a **vegetating spirit** <note>term: "spiritus vegetans" — in 17th-century vitalist philosophy, the subtle spirit that gives life and growth to bodies</note> common to both dwells and resides secretly. And you ought also to note here (honest reader) that in this particular book of my *Mystical Anatomy*, I have treated the secret and occult properties of the spiritual or internal blood within the external, citing at the same time, according to the capacity of my wit, the harmonic effects which it works both by immediate contact or touch, and at a distance. Indeed, I would like to know where and in what I have offended by doing so, or how I have deserved Mr. Foster's offensive entry into the examination of this business; or whether, in my natural discourse upon this subject, I bring forward any Diabolical deceptions, circles, sorcery, or forbidden and illicit **characters** <note>term: "characteres" — magical symbols or sigils often used in ritual magic</note> and the like? But if you detect nothing that pertains to any such Diabolical Magic, give your judgment as to whether such a prelude to this business was honest, decent, or in any way relevant to the matter in hand. As for the use of this **Weapon-Salve** <note>original: "Unguenti armarij"</note>, I protest before God and man that I have never even practiced it to this very day; but in my conscience and by reason of a more strict inquiry than I have applied to it for this cause of verifying it, I find it so free from any Diabolical superstition (which, God is my witness, I have always hated, as I also hate the Devil and all his works). And I have heard so much of its virtuous operation that, in the future, despite the envy of inhuman writers and babblers against it and its practice, I intend to use it and defend its lawful manner, being now more certain than ever before that it is the blessed power of God, and no act of the Devil, since it works for the health and relief of God's afflicted creatures.

->But, to return to our matter.<-

I will finish the matter in "three words" (as they say); for I have already been too long-winded in my preceding discourse. But before I make a beginning, I ask you to observe, benevolent reader, that our **sponge-bearer** <note>original: "spongifer," Fludd’s nickname for Foster, who "carried a sponge" to wipe away Fludd's ideas</note> is quite halting and imperfect in the interpretation of my Text, stretching himself far from its genuine and true nature so that he may retain his own sense, rather than faithfully expressing my intention (as is proper, by Hercules!). In the first place, therefore, I will set out for you in bare words the full and exact scope of my Latin Text, which I call my **assertion**, and then afterwards, in the next place, I will unfold its exposition or collection. Here and there I shall depict the virtuous efficacy...

# THE FOSTERIAN SPONGES.

...his sponges <note>A reference to Foster’s book title, *The Sponge*, which aimed to "wipe away" the reputation of the weapon-salve.</note> in absorbing, devouring, or destroying the force of my assertion: And then, in the final place, I shall squeeze his sponge, forcing it to vomit up again the truth which it had devoured—or rather, which it had covered with its veil of ignorance. And this shall be our manner of proceeding in fighting against this **Lernean monster** <note>A mythical multi-headed hydra; Fludd suggests Foster's arguments are numerous and toxic.</note> and this truth-devouring sponge.

### CHAPTER I.

<margin>Here it is proven, against the assertion of our censor:</margin>
> First, that the blood, fat, flesh, and bones of a dead man participate in that **Balsamic nature** <note>The inherent healing principle in living tissue.</note> or **radical moisture** <note>The fundamental vital fluid believed by alchemists to sustain life and resist decay.</note> which exists in a living man. Second, that a horse possesses a **Balsam** that sympathizes with that of a man.

### *My own bare text.*

We see that this ointment is composed of things that are in every way concordant with human nature, and consequently, it has a great regard for its health and preservation: Inasmuch as for its composition, we have in the primary place **blood**, in which the power of life resides: Here, I say, is the essence of human bones, growing out of them in the form of a moss called **usnea** <note>original: "usnea." In early modern medicine, this referred specifically to moss gathered from the skull of a man who died a violent death.</note>: Here is his flesh in the **Mummy** <note>original: "Mummia." Medicinal mummy was a substance made from preserved or desiccated human remains, highly prized in the 17th-century pharmacopeia.</note>, which is composed of flesh and Balsam: Here is the fat of the human body, which joins with the rest for the perfection of this Ointment: And with all these (as has been said) blood is mixed, which was the principle and nourishment of them all: Inasmuch as the spirit of life is in it, and with it the **luminous soul** dwells, operating in a hidden manner: So that the entire perfection of the human body seems to join together for the making of this precious Ointment. And this is the reason why there is such great respect and consensus between this ointment and the blood of the wounded person: For it is quite necessary that the blood of the wounded person be drawn from the depth of the wound, etc.: and this is the exact text of my meaning.

### *Now you shall see how highly he regards it.*

### *Mr. Foster’s Collection.*

**Usnea** or bones (says *Doctor Fludd*), **mummy**, and human fat (special ingredients) comprise the corporal perfection of man, and thus are fit and ready for consolidating <note>Healing or closing a wound.</note>, by reason of the **natural Balsam** resting in them, which sympathizes with the **hypostatic Balsam** <note>The essential, underlying healing spirit.</note> residing in a living man. Later, when I speak of that Balsamic ingredient which the nobles of *Egypt* used to make from natural Balsam and similar bituminous and oily things (which are enemies to corruption), he interprets it according to his own fashion and to satisfy himself, saying: that I speak of the natural Balsam in man, residing in the ointment, etc.: But indeed, I will grant him his aim, since he cannot significantly vary from my purpose and intent.

### *Fludd.*

You see, first, that he omits the blood, which is the chief ingredient, and then—

### *Mr. Foster’s sponge for deleting this assertion of mine.*

### *Foster.*

I deny that **usnea** or bones, **mummy**, and human fat—though they may be medicinal—possess any **natural Balsam** or **radical moisture** (for so some call natural Balsam) residing in them, which sympathizes with the hypostatic Balsam remaining in a living man, unless a horse has a Balsam sympathizing with that of a man: For (says *Dr. Fludd*, which I advise you to keep well in memory) if a nail that pricks a horse is put into a vessel of the Ointment, the horse will be cured: But I say, there is no such sympathy between a horse and a man, and if there is no cause for us to believe the one, there is little cause to believe the other.

->Here the sponge is squeezed.<-

### *Fludd.*

Observe in the first place, that our censor forgets that blood is among the ingredients; and then he disputes from things not conceded, as was said before: and yet, nevertheless, I will grant him his way, and I will prove that everything his spongy tongue has brought forth for the deletion and extirpation of that truth which was expressed by me here, is of no efficacy, nor even of any appearance or probability. Therefore, I will divide this refutation of his into two branches or members, the first of which offers this question: namely, whether blood, flesh, fat, and bones have any balsam...

He rebukes William Foster for omitting blood from his critique and for mocking the idea of animal-human sympathy in healing.</summary>

Fludd is arguing that life-giving principles—specifically "balsam" and "radical moisture"—persist in biological matter even after it has been separated from a living body, a key pillar of his defense for the "weapon-salve" (a sympathetic ointment).</meta>

### EXPRESSION OF PART III.

...natural balsam or **radical moisture** <term>radical moisture</term> <note>The fundamental vital fluid believed by alchemists to sustain life and resist decay.</note> residing in itself, sympathizing with the **Hypostatic Balsam** <note>The underlying spiritual essence of healing.</note> remaining with the living man? The latter question asks: whether a horse possesses a **Balsam** that sympathizes with that of a man?

The first of these is established as absolutely negative by our Foster; but I, with more certain confidence, establish it as affirmative, and I shall provide proof of it in a three-fold way: first, by natural reason; second, by the authority of the Sacred Scriptures; and finally, by common experience.

In the first place, therefore, I would wish our Foster to learn what a **balsamic nature** is, before he so precipitately and preposterously prepares to censure creatures by claiming they are destitute of it. Wherefore, I must point out to him that it is nothing other than an **essential and volatile salt**, which abounds with vegetative and multiplicative power. This power it receives from above, like a precious soul, to give life and animation. This power is that **innate heat** or natural heat, by the virtue of which every living creature exists; and the volatile vehicle in which it is carried is that **radical moisture**, through which and in which the said power moves immediately and acts toward life, growth, and multiplication. Therefore, by the essential active and passive operation of these, vegetables and animals clearly grow and multiply—and minerals do so hiddenly. This occurs as much in their form (or natural fire) as in their substance. For this reason, the true Alchemists call this mystic salt the **Sun of the Wise** <note>original: "Sol Sapientum." An alchemical term for the universal spirit or the "philosophical gold" that animates nature.</note>, in which the whole mystery of nature consists; and others call it the true **Balsam of nature**. Hence, the wiser Philosophers affirm that whatever the wise seek is found in that salt. As far as the airy part is concerned, it is that volatile salt which is expanded everywhere in the open air; it is the purest essence from and in the air, in which the grain of life lies hidden. And so other wise men say: "In the air is the hidden food of life," etc.

<margin>
Matt. 5.
Matt. 5.
</margin>
Nor is it without a mystic and secret cause that our Savior specially considered **Salt**, saying in a certain place: *You are the salt of the earth*, by which he understands the spiritual man, in whom is the spirit of life. And again: *If the salt has lost its savor, wherewith shall it be salted? It is good for nothing anymore, but to be cast out and trodden under foot by men.* By these words it is clear that nothing can be, exist, or be of any value or esteem without this mystic salt or **Glue of life**, but would be utterly dead and corrupt. There is Salt even in the dung-heap or manure, which provides life and soul to the earth, which multiplies the grain in greater proportion and draws to itself much more abundantly the celestial influence of life.

Lastly: the very essence of the blood of animals in general consists in this **Balsamic salt**. Through it the body is animated; through it the flesh—by the positioning, union, and gluing together of parts—is vivified, multiplied, and successively preserved. Through this salt, the blood in a man is daily increased by the bread and flesh of other creatures. In this, therefore, is the incorruptible spirit of life which sustains the life of man and delivers him from corruption; and unless it performed its life-giving duty, man would quickly putrefy and be <margin>Levit. 17.</margin> corrupted. For why do the Scriptures confirm so much in many places—namely, that the **soul of man is in the blood**? Now it is certain that this vivifying spirit, which is the gift of God to every creature (as proven before), is the true operator within this radical and moist Tabernacle, healing, restoring, and consolidating wounds, aided by any application made either by real or virtual contact. Therefore, it follows that this spirit—lying hidden in the blood, and in the fat, bones, and human flesh—participates in this spirit which, as Scripture says, animates and heals all things: <margin>Wis. 16:12.</margin> *For it was neither herb nor emollient that healed them,* says Solomon, *but thy Word, O Lord, which healeth all things.*

But Master Foster will object that this spirit of life is in the blood, fat, and flesh only when it is not separated from the living man; but after it is separated, it possesses nothing more of life or essence. I have told him, and proved the contrary in my philosophical demonstration: for without this Salt and the living spirit within it, neither blood, fat, nor bones could hold together. Rather, according to that saying of Christ cited before, they would be of no use. Conversely, it is maintained in sacred writings that the spirit of life is in their central or interior parts, even if it does not act or work but rests in the center, as I have clearly declared before. For otherwise, why would it be said: *You shall entirely abstain from eating blood and fat*? And again: *You shall not take the blood of a creature into <margin>Lev. 3. Lev. 7. Lev. 17.</margin> your food*? And again: *The blood of a wild beast or bird killed in the hunt must be poured out upon the earth*; and the reason is given there, *because the spirit of life is in the blood*; and again it is said, *the soul of the flesh is in the blood*. Now, if the spirit of life vanished from the blood, flesh, fat, and bones immediately after their separation from the living creature, what need would there be for such a wealth of words or such strict precepts not to eat of the blood and fat after the death of the creatures? Or why would that reason be given? Because the soul or life *is* in the blood, or the blood *is* the seat of the soul and life? The text does not say: "The blood *was* the seat of the soul or life," but it *is*: namely, the substance of those parts, though separated from the living body, still participates in the spirit of life. Where-

F 2 <meta>catchword: -fore (Latin: propter)</meta>

<summary>Fludd argues that a "mystic salt" or "radical moisture" acts as the vehicle for the spirit of life, persisting in blood and tissue even after death. He uses alchemical theory and Biblical prohibitions against eating blood to prove that life-giving properties remain in separated biological matter.</summary>
