<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s argument that biological matter retains a latent vital power after death or separation. He is responding to William Foster’s claim that using an ointment made of human ingredients (like blood or "usnea," the moss from a skull) is either superstitious or demonic. Fludd uses biblical precedents and chemical observations to prove that a "life-giving spirit" persists in these substances.</meta>

# FOSTER’S SPONGES.

...therefore take care for yourselves, that you do not eat of them. <note>original: "propter cavete vobis, ne comedatis ex illis." This likely refers to biblical prohibitions against consuming blood or certain fats.</note>

<margin>2 Kings 13.</margin>
<margin>Revelation 6:9.</margin>
<margin>Genesis 4:10.</margin>
I will not recall to your memory here that life-giving power remaining with the bones of Elisha <note>original: "Eliæ" (Elijah), though the biblical reference 2 Kings 13:21 refers to Elisha's bones reviving a dead man.</note>, which caused a murdered body, thrown by thieves into the tomb of Elisha, to live; nor that the souls of those who had been slain for the sake of the Word cried out to the Lord from beneath the altar for vengeance; nor that the voice of Abel's shed blood cried out to the Lord from the earth; nor the sudden re-animation of dead blood in a slain man in the presence of the murderer or killer—which could not happen unless this life-giving spirit participated in and lay hidden within the blood, albeit without action, until it was excited to action by the spirit of the killer, etc. 

But I will lead you to visual experience: It is as certain as can be to those who have applied their minds to the art of distillation, that the blood and bones of a man contain a remarkable quantity of **volatile salt** <term>sal volatile</term> <note>A chemical essence extracted from organic matter, believed to house the vital spirit.</note>, in which resides such an excellent **Balsamic** <note>Healing or preservative</note> disposition that, by reason of its closeness to nature, it soothes gouty pains as well as other intolerable torments, cures wounds, and heals those suffering from uterine afflictions and epilepsy. Furthermore, experience has made it manifest that the volatile salt and oil of blood are a distinguished cordial <note>A medicine that invigorates the heart.</note>. Conversely, frequent experience has taught both my masters and myself that oil from human blood alleviates gouty and other similar pains, heals wounds, and, as if in an instant, cleanses and dries up all kinds of excoriations <note>Skin abrasions or scrapings.</note>. 

Do we not see a single drop from a candle heal a scrape in a single night? And every stable-hand will make it known to himself that a horse’s heel, wounded or struck by a stone or hoof, is cured by anointing it with the end of a candle. No surgeon will deny that hog’s fat and deer tallow are proven necessities as ingredients for a healing ointment. And from where does this healing property in them proceed? What? From a benign act of God, or from the Devil? If from the former, it is from that healing and life-giving spirit which first formed these members and granted them this power; for otherwise, they would possess no curative property. <margin>Psalm 33.</margin> "By the spirit from the mouth of God," says David, "all power proceeds." Therefore, from that spirit, the fat, flesh, blood, and bones obtained that power of healing—or they did not obtain it at all—and His presence always preserves that power within them; indeed, even after their separation or amputation from the living body, they retain what they received while they were members in a living body. 

The only difference is this: when they were in the living body, their power was **actual**; but when separated, it is merely **potential**, and it will not be brought back into action unless it is incited by that very same life-giving and activating spirit. Just as we see fat or tallow become fixed with cold and unable to flow, but with the act of natural heat or fire, it immediately melts and flows. What will our sponge-bearing critic <note>Referring to William Foster, who wrote "The Squeezing of Foster's Sponge."</note> reply to all this? Is it not therefore necessary that the spirit of life be within this? For without it, there can be no **sympathy** <term>sympathizatio</term> <note>The occult connection between two things that allows them to affect each other at a distance.</note> between this and the **Hypostatic Balsam** <term>Hypostatic Balsam</term> <note>The fundamental healing essence or spiritual "blueprint" residing in the living person.</note> residing in the living man. 

I must make it known to this inquisitor that just as there was only one <margin>Ezekiel 37.</margin> spirit which was called by the Prophet from the four winds to breathe life into the slain, so there is only one spirit that gives power to the living blood, flesh, fat, and bones, as well as to the rest of the things that seem to us to be destitute of life or only in potential toward action. There is only one spirit, but diverse in its properties, which congeals and, as it were, kills the spirit of the mobile element of air, fixing it with the North wind into snow, frost, ice, and hail, and revives it again with the South wind. It will be of no use to our critic if he excludes this spirit from the fat, blood, flesh, and **skull-moss** <note>original: "usnea." A moss that grows on the skulls of dead men, used in the weapon-salve.</note> which enter into the ointment. For the Wise Man <margin>Wisdom 12.</margin> says: "An incorruptible spirit is in all things"; therefore, it is in this ointment. 

We have, therefore, a **Balsamic Salt** in all these ingredients, and in that salt the active power lies hidden. This power, through the priority of the source of action, flowing and acting from the "point from which," reacts toward the "point to which"—that is, from the end back to the beginning. And this is the reason why this ointment heals not only by real contact but also by **virtual contact** <note>Healing through the "virtue" or power of the spirit acting at a distance.</note>, namely by reason of that power which it holds from its first Creator. It is as if one were to say a herb or a root loses all healing power because it was gathered from a plant; or that a grain of wheat, or an apple gathered from the stalk or tree, does not have Balsamic nourishment in itself because it has passed its time for further growth. And yet the contrary is manifested, insofar as they always possess within themselves their own vegetating and multiplying spirit. For when that grain is thrown into the earth, the very atom of life lying hidden within it manifests itself, making it grow and multiply according to its species. Nor indeed are the meats of beasts destitute of their nutritive property, though they seem dead and are separated from the living creature. For the Scripture says: "The soul of the flesh is in the blood." If this were not so, it would not nourish or be converted into the bodily substance of a man—namely, blood, flesh, fat, and bone. Just as, unless a life-giving spirit lay hidden in the flesh of a dead corpse, it would be impossible for it to be converted into worms through its exposure to the solar rays, as will be discussed hereafter.

Lastly, I could show this profound philosopher that this life-giving spirit is produced abundantly in **volatile salt**. Indeed, I could demonstrate to him visually how it draws down the form of life from the Sun...

<summary>Fludd argues that a "life-giving spirit" remains in biological matter like blood, bone, and fat after death, using biblical examples (like Elisha's bones) and alchemical experiments with "volatile salts" to justify the weapon-salve's efficacy through spiritual sympathy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s defense of the "Weapon Salve" against his critic, William Foster. Fludd is arguing that biological substances like blood and salts contain a "vegetable spirit" or "balsam" that links all living things through a shared life force, even after death.</meta>

### EXPRESSION OF SECTION III

...by the Sun, such that from a translucent **volatile salt** <term>sal volatile: a chemical essence believed to carry the life-force</term>, which resembles snow in its whiteness, or from a crystalline, oily, flowing liquid, it might turn as red as a ruby within a few hours simply by exposing it to the solar rays. This is the **sympathy** <note>In Fludd’s philosophy, "sympathy" is a natural attraction or resonance between two things sharing the same essence.</note> between that substance and the form of the Sun; in that very category is their reciprocal appetite, much like that between an agent and a patient, or a female and a male. I could even demonstrate, within a very short interval of time, the admirable power which this **vegetable spirit** possesses to produce growth in all things. I could also prove it to be a most health-giving **Balsam** <term>Balsam: a life-preserving fluid or internal healing agent</term> for curing wounds and soothing pains. Therefore, it sympathizes with the hypostatic balsam of man; otherwise, it would not be converted into the same image—namely, into blood, flesh, fat, and bones. Consequently, the blood, flesh, fat, and bones of the same species are much more compatible, since like is most nourished by its like. 

Do we not see that human blood—indeed, the blood of every creature—consists of such a volatile salt? Even if there were nothing but urine, which is the watery excrement of blood, it would still testify to this, since it is moderately full of **sal ammoniac** <term>sal armoniacum: an ammonium salt often used in alchemy</term> or animal volatile salt. By reason of its Balsamic nature, human urine is so well-suited for cleansing and curing a simple, freshly inflicted wound that even jaundice can be cured at a distance from the patient in such a manner, as has been declared previously. You see, therefore, with what ease—and from three different perspectives—this stupid **"sponge" of Foster’s** <note>A mocking reference to William Foster's book "A Sponge to wipe away the Weapon-Salve," which attacked Fludd's theories.</note> is squeezed dry, and how irrational and improbable his aforementioned proposition is. I come now to the examination of the second question that arises from this.

As for the second question—namely, whether a horse possesses a Balsam that sympathizes with that of a man? Mr. Foster says there is no such sympathy between a horse and a man. He blathers much and babbles rashly, but proves little or nothing, as if to say: "This is the will of Mr. Foster, and therefore let his will stand for reason." In this, he exactly imitates his boasting teacher, **Mersenne** <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a famous French polymath and priest who was a fierce critic of Fludd’s Hermetic ideas.</note>. But I shall take up the boldness to instruct him better in this business, and I will demonstrate to him that the corporeal nature of one creature is easily drawn into sympathy with and participates in the nature of another. For the flesh, fat, and bones of both are made of blood in a natural generality—indeed, even in a specific sense they are from blood, even if they vary in number. For I ask you: is not the same flesh, fat, and blood of a beast accustomed to nourish its like in a man? Is not one transmuted into the other? Not at all? Does not the Scripture say this in a general sense—meaning all blood—namely, that the soul or life of the creature is in the blood, and the life of all flesh is in the blood? <note>original: "animam seu vitam creaturæ esse in sanguine & vitam omnis carnis esse in sanguine" — a reference to Leviticus 17:11.</note> And because of the divine respect for that spirit of life in the blood, are we not forbidden from eating the blood of any creature? And furthermore:

Human blood, in a reciprocal respect, must be required from the beast that shed it. <note>original: "Sanguis humanus in respectu reciproco repeti debet à bestia" — a reference to Genesis 9:5.</note> When all these things are rightly weighed, which of the wise men can call it into doubt, or fail to conclude absolutely, that the corporeal nature of a beast sympathizes and corresponds with the parts of the human body? I confess, indeed, that the intellectual nature of man makes him differ from a horse, insofar as man is called a **rational animal** and the beast an **irrational animal**. However, these properties are only seen in the *specific spirit* and do not concern or touch any action of life, or growth, or multiplication, or healing. Therefore, I argue in this way: God gave man a double gift. The first is the spirit of life, which He imparted not only to man but also to all other creatures. And yet, He bestowed more upon man than any other creature, for He gave him intellect. Nevertheless, the giver of this double gift is only one spirit. Hence, <margin>Job 33[:4]</margin> Job says:
> The Spirit of God has made me, and the breath of the Almighty has given me life.

Now, therefore (as I said), this same beneficial gift was given to all other creatures in one property and office, whence it is said: "God gives life to all things." And <margin>Judith 16[:14]. Isaiah 42:5.</margin> Judith says:
> He sent his spirit and created all things.

And Isaiah:
> God gives breath to the people and spirit to those who walk upon the earth.

From these it is clear that the same spirit of life—proportionally, though differing in number, measure, and proportion—is poured out upon every specific animal. Therefore, an admirable sympathy of nature must necessarily exist between the parts of any animal, which are produced through growth and multiplication by the operation of that same spirit of life infused into the blood, and thus by way of animation into the fat, flesh, and bones. And this is the reason that like is converted into like: namely, blood into blood, flesh into blood and flesh, and fat into its like, and bones and marrow are fashioned from both. Is it not quite palpable that any flesh, blood, or fat of dead animals is converted by the change of digestion into the substance of a man? This could never be achieved unless they sympathized excellently with one another in nature, and united their Balsamic nature—that is, their **innate heat** and **radical moisture** <term>calidum innatum & humidum radicale: the two vital principles of life in Galenic medicine</term>—mutually, and transmuted the substance of one into that of the other, which is blood originally, both manifest and hidden. But as for the other extraordinary gift, it is said by <margin>Job 32[:8]</margin> Job elsewhere:
> In man there is a spirit, but the inspiration of the Almighty makes him understand.

Therefore, the intellect is a separate gift that makes man differ from the beast, but the spirit of life does not.

# FOSTER’S SPONGES

...because of this, take care that you do not eat of them. <note>original: "propter cavete vobis, ne comedatis ex illis." This likely refers to biblical dietary laws regarding blood.</note>

<margin>2 Kings 13; Revelation 6:9; Genesis 4:10</margin>
I will not recall to your memory here the life-giving power remaining with the bones of **Elisha** <note>In 2 Kings 13:21, a dead body touched Elisha's bones and returned to life.</note>, which caused the slain body—thrown into Elisha’s tomb by those thieves—to revive. Nor will I remind you that the souls of those who were slain for the sake of the Word cried out to the Lord from beneath the altar for vengeance; nor that the voice of **Abel’s** shed blood cried out to the Lord from the earth; nor the sudden revival of dead blood in a slain man in the presence of the homicide or killer. This last event could not happen unless this life-giving spirit participated and lay hidden within the blood, albeit without action, until it was stirred into action by the "slayer-spirit" <note>Fludd refers to "cruentation," the folklore belief that a victim's wounds bleed in the presence of their murderer.</note> &c.

But I shall lead you to ocular experience: It is as certain as can be to those who have applied their minds to the **art of distillation** <note>The early chemical process of extracting "essences" from matter.</note>, that the blood and bones of a man contain a remarkable quantity of **volatile salt** <term>volatile salt</term> <note>In alchemy, a crystalline substance extracted from organic matter, believed to hold its vital essence.</note>, in which resides such an excellent **Balsamic disposition** that, by reason of its proximity to nature, it soothes gouty pains and other intolerable torments immediately. It cures wounds, heals those suffering from afflictions of the womb, and treats epilepsy. Toward the end of the process, experience has made it manifest that the volatile salt and the oil of blood are a remarkable **cordial** <note>A medicine intended to invigorate the heart and spirit.</note>. 

In turn, frequent experience has taught both my masters and myself that oil from human blood alleviates gouty and other similar pains, heals wounds, and, as if in an instant, cleanses and dries out every kind of excoriation <note>Skin abrasions or sores.</note>. Do we not see a single drop from a candle heal a sore in one night? And any stable-hand will make it known to you that a horse's heel, wounded or struck by a stone or hoof, is cured by the application of the end of a candle. No surgeon will deny that pig lard and deer suet are proven necessary ingredients for a **healing ointment**. And from where does this healing property in them proceed? What? From a benign act of God, or from the Devil? If from the former, it is from that healing and life-giving spirit which first formed these limbs and granted them this power; for otherwise, it would possess no curative property.

<margin>Psalm 33</margin>
"By the spirit from the mouth of God," says David, "all power proceeds." Therefore, from that spirit, the fat, flesh, blood, and bones obtained that power of healing—or they did not obtain it at all. And by His presence, they always preserve that power from Him; indeed, even after their separation or amputation from the living body, they retain what they received from Him while they were members in a living body. 

There is only this difference: when they were in the living body, their power was **actual**, but once separated, it is only **potential**. It does not wish to be reduced into action unless it is incited by that very same life-giving and actuating spirit. Just as we see fat or suet become fixed and solid with the cold, not being fluid, but with the act of natural heat or fire, it immediately liquefies and flows. What will our "sponge-bearing" critic <note>Fludd's nickname for William Foster, who wrote "The Squeezing of Paracelsus' Sponge."</note> reply to all this? Surely, then, the spirit of life must be present in this matter; for without it, there can be no **sympathy** between this and the **hypostatic balsam** <note>The essential healing fluid.</note> residing in a living man. 

I must make it known to this <margin>Ezekiel 37</margin> inquirer that, just as there was only one spirit which was called by the Prophet from the four winds to breathe life into the slain, so there is only one spirit that gives power to living blood, flesh, fat, and bones, as well as to the rest of the things which seem to us to be destitute of life or in a state of potentiality. There is only one spirit, but it is diverse in its properties. It congeals and, as it were, kills the spirit of the moving element of air, fixing it with a Northern blast into snow, frost, ice, and hail, and then revives it again with a Southern blast. 

It will be of no use to our critic if he excludes this spirit from the fat, blood, flesh, and **usnea** <term>usnea</term> <note>Moss grown on the skull of a person who died a violent death, a key ingredient in the weapon-salve.</note> which enter into the ointment. For the Wise Man says: <margin>Wisdom 12</margin> "An incorruptible spirit is in all things." Therefore, it is in this ointment. 

We have, therefore, a **Balsamic Salt** in all these ingredients, and in that salt lies hidden an active power. This power, through the fountain of action, flowing and acting from the "starting point," reacts toward the "endpoint"—that is, from the end back to the beginning. And this is the reason why this ointment cures not only by real contact but also by **virtual contact** <note>Healing at a distance through spiritual or "virtual" influence.</note>, specifically by reason of that power which it holds from its first Creator. It is as if one were to say a herb or root loses all healing power because it has been gathered from the plant. Surely a grain of wheat, or an apple gathered from the stalk or tree, does not lose its **Balsamic nourishment** just because its time for further growth has passed? And yet the contrary is shown, insofar as they always possess within themselves their own vegetating and multiplying spirit. For when that grain is cast into the earth, the very atom of life hidden within it manifests itself, causing it to grow and multiply according to its kind. 

Truly, the flesh of beasts is not destitute of its nutritional property, even though they seem dead and are separated from the living creature. For the Scripture says: "The soul of the flesh is in the blood." If this were not so, it would not nourish or be converted into the substance of a man—namely into blood, flesh, fat, and bones. Likewise, unless a life-giving spirit lay hidden in the flesh of a dead corpse, it would be impossible for it to be converted into worms through its exposure to the rays of the sun, as will be discussed hereafter.

Finally, I could show to this "profound" philosopher how this life-giving spirit is produced abundantly in **volatile salt**. Indeed, I could demonstrate to his eyes how it sucks down the form of life from the Sun...

# EXPRESSION OF PART III.

Fol. 23.
F 3
<meta>catchword: What</meta>

...from the Sun, such that from a transparent volatile salt, resembling snow in its whiteness, or a flowing oily crystalline liquor, it becomes as red as a ruby within a few hours by exposing it to the solar rays. This is the sympathy between it and the form of the **Sun**, and in that very kind is their reciprocal appetite, such as exists between agent and patient, or female and male. I could also show in a very short interval of time the admirable power which this vegetable spirit <note>In this context, "vegetable" refers to the power of growth and biological life, not specifically to plants.</note> possesses to produce growth in all things; and it can also be proven to be a most healthy **Balsam** <note>A life-preserving essence or healing substance believed to exist within living bodies.</note> for curing wounds and soothing pains: and therefore it sympathizes with the hypostatic balsam of man; for otherwise it would not be converted into the same image—namely, into blood, flesh, fat, and bones—and therefore much more the blood, flesh, fat, and bones of one and the same species, since like is nourished by its like. Do we not see that human blood—indeed, the blood of every creature—consists of such a volatile salt? If there were nothing besides urine, which is the serous <note>Watery or thin.</note> excrement of the blood, it would indeed testify as much, since it abounds moderately in **Armoniac** salt <term>sal armoniacum</term> <note>Sal ammoniac, a salt Fludd associates here with the "volatile salt" found in animal life.</note> or animal volatile salt: and by reason of its Balsamic nature, human urine is so suited for cleansing and curing a simple freshly inflicted wound, that even **jaundice** <note>original: "icterus"</note> is cured at such a distance from the patient, as has already been declared before. You see, therefore, with what ease—and that by a triple consideration—the stupid sponge of our **Foster** <note>Fludd is mocking the title of William Foster's book, *The Hoplocrisma-Spongus*, which aimed to "wipe away" the weapon-salve as a superstition.</note> is wrung out, and how dissonant from reason and improbable his aforementioned proposition is. I come, therefore, to the examination of the second question which arises from this.

<margin>Job 33. Judith 16. Isaiah 42:5. Job 32.</margin>
As for the second question—namely, whether a horse has a **Balsam** sympathizing with that of a man? **Mr. Foster** says there is no such sympathy between a horse and a man: he babbles much and prattles rashly, but proves little or nothing, as if he were saying: "This is **Mr. Foster's** will, and therefore let his will stand for reason." He exactly imitates his boasting Master, **Mersenne** <note>Marin Mersenne, a French philosopher who was a prominent critic of Fludd’s mystical and alchemical theories.</note>. But I shall clothe myself with such boldness as to instruct him better in this business, and I will demonstrate to him that the corporeal nature of one is easily drawn into sympathy and participates with that of another: for the flesh, fat, and bones of both are from blood in natural generality—indeed, even in specialty from blood, though they vary in number. For I ask you, is not the same flesh, fat, and blood of a beast accustomed to nourish the like in man? Is not one transmuted into the other? By no means? Does not the Scripture say in this general sense—meaning all blood—namely, that the soul or life of the creature is in the blood, and the life of all flesh is in the blood, and that because of the divine respect for the spirit of that life in the blood, we are forbidden to eat the blood of any creature? and again:

Human blood, in reciprocal respect, ought to be demanded back from the beast that shed it. When all these things are skillfully weighed, which of the Wise can call it into doubt, or not absolutely conclude, that the corporal nature of a beast sympathizes and corresponds with the parts of the human body? I confess indeed that the intellectual nature of man causes him to differ from that of a horse, insofar as the former is said to be a **rational animal**, while the beast is an **irrational animal**; but these properties are only seen in the specific spirit, and they do not concern or touch any action of life, or vegetation, or multiplication, or healing. Therefore, I shall reason in this manner: God endowed man with a double gift, the first of which is the spirit of life, which He imparted not only to him but also to all other creatures. And again, He bestowed upon him more than upon any other creature: for He gave him understanding: and yet the giver of this double gift is only one spirit: and hence **Job** says: *The Spirit of God made me, and the inspiration of the Almighty gave me life.* Now, therefore (as I have said), this same beneficial gift was given to all other creatures in one property and office: whence it is said: *God gives life to all things;* and **Judith**: *He sent his spirit and created all things.* And **Isaiah**: *God gives breath to the people and spirit to those who walk upon the earth.* From which it is clear that the same spirit of life, proportionally—though in different ways in number, measure, and proportion—is poured out upon every specific animal: and therefore there must be an admirable sympathy of nature between the parts of any animal, which are produced by growth and multiplication through the operation of the same spirit of life infused into the blood: and so through the path of animation to the fat, flesh, and bones. And this is the reason why like is converted into like—namely, blood into blood, flesh into blood and flesh, and fat into its like, and bones and marrow are forged from both. Is it not quite palpable that any flesh or blood, or fat of dead animals, is converted by the change of digestion into the substance of a man? Which could never be achieved unless they sympathized excellently with each other in nature, and united their **Balsamic** nature—or innate heat and radical moisture <note>Standard medical concepts of the time: innate heat was the body's internal fire, and radical moisture was the life-sustaining fluid that fueled it.</note>—mutually, and transmuted the substance of one into that of the other, which is blood originally, as much manifestly as hiddenly. But as for the other extraordinary gift, it is said by **Job** elsewhere: *There is a spirit in man, but the inspiration of the Almighty makes him understand:* understanding, therefore, is a separate gift, which makes man differ from the beast: but not the spirit of life.

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s defense of the "Weapon Salve" against his critic, William Foster. Having previously discussed the "volatile salt" and "vegetable spirit" found in blood and urine, Fludd now addresses Foster’s specific skepticism regarding the "sympathy" between humans and animals, specifically horses.</meta>

<center>### FOSTER’S SPONGES <note>original: "SPONGIÆ FOSTERIANÆ"</note> ###</center>

<margin>Page 39.</margin>
What therefore remains? "The Doctor must remember well, etc.," says Mr. Foster.

And what must he remember? Given such a strict warning from a "wise man," it must surely involve a matter of great weight. "He should remember," Foster says, "his own cure of horses." And what cure of horses? Namely, that a horse, when a nail is badly driven in, can be cured in the same way. A marvelous work! Was it for this difficult reason that the same reminder was repeated in the "Glorious and Famous" part of his "Sponge," in order to erase my assertion? Let us therefore contemplate the chief subject of his reminder, which is this:

<margin>Page 42.</margin>
For the Doctor says (which I warned him to remember) that if the nail which injured the horse is applied to the **Ointment** <term>Unguentum: the Weapon Salve, applied to the object that caused the wound rather than the wound itself</term>, the horse will be cured; yet I say there is no such **sympathy** <note>In this context, "sympathy" refers to a natural, invisible connection between two things that allows them to affect one another at a distance.</note> between a man and a horse.

Ha ha ha! Can you hold back your laughter, friends? <note>original: "Ha ha he! Risum teneatis amici?" — a reference to the Roman poet Horace</note> He says it is so, therefore the matter must be so. His will stands in place of reason: he speaks, and though he proves nothing, he expects to be believed. But this man’s assertion will be proven ridiculous, as much by common and vulgar observation as by the various practical experiments of the Nobleman, or Count, whom I cited in Chapter 6, Section 2 of this treatise.

As far as common observation is concerned, we say that the flesh of all creatures (as I have said before), whether they be birds or quadrupeds, and for that reason horses, is easily converted (after it has been digested in the human stomach) into human blood, fat, and bone. This is an evident argument that there is a manifest sympathy between the flesh and blood of a horse and that of a man; indeed, there resides in the horse a **Balsamic nature** <term>Balsamic nature: the internal, life-preserving power of an organism that enables healing and growth</term> similar to the **radical moisture** <term>radical moisture: the essential fluid believed by medieval and Renaissance doctors to be the fundamental fuel of life</term> which is in man. Consequently, that same Balsamic nature sympathizes with the **hypostatic balsam** <term>hypostatic balsam: the underlying, essential healing essence within a living body</term> residing in man. The cause is manifest: for that which makes something a certain way is itself even more that way. Therefore, if the blood or flesh of a horse were not of the same nature as that of a man, it would never be converted and reduced into union with human blood and flesh. Even a fool perceives the truth of this in practice. From this it is clear that the Balsamic nature of one exactly agrees with the other; otherwise, they would never be so **homogeneous** <note>Meaning "of the same kind" or "compatible in substance."</note> as to comprise a single nature. And again: if they did not sympathize, but rather felt **Antipathy** <note>The natural repulsion between two opposing forces.</note>, the nature of one would shrink from the nature of the other, which experience shows to be false.

Again, the effect proves that there is a Balsamic nature in the horse which sympathizes with that of man: for the effect of the Balsamic nature is to glue together and consolidate wounds, and to create flesh and generate blood, and this by the secret power of **vegetation** <note>In the 17th century, "vegetation" referred to the principle of life, growth, and healing shared by plants, animals, and humans.</note>. But the flesh of the horse yields and delivers its Balsamic juice to the liver, where it sympathizes with its nature, so that it yields itself by a homogeneous transmutation into blood, and is rendered equally fibrous and properly compacted as the rest of the human blood, and is finally made one with it. And after this by "apposition, union, and assimilation" (to use the words of **Galen** <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–216 AD), the most influential physician of antiquity whose theories dominated Western medicine for 1,500 years.</note>) it becomes human flesh. This is an infallible argument that the Balsamic nature of these two creatures agrees and sympathizes, otherwise they could not achieve a single union. Thus our sharp-witted "remembrancer" <note>Foster.</note> can see that I do not only speak in this manner, but I also prove my cause so tangibly that every simple person may perceive it through their senses.

I come now to those private experiments which that Noble Count I cited before found to be true in horses: some of which had nails badly driven into them, and others were wounded or injured otherwise. He was pleased to relate to me many histories of his cures, both in his own horses and in those of others, which he had performed himself by the virtue of this Ointment. Now, I would truly like to know whether any man of good reputation or endowed with modesty would rather give credit to those things which this nobleman affirms and wishes to testify upon his own knowledge and various experience, or to the naked and empty assertion of Mr. Foster? Foster wants to persuade the world, by his mere assertion alone without any further proof or practice, that "castles can be built in the air." What then? Shall we call a meeting of impetuous, incredulous, and critical men to decide it? Does the **Devil** perform this cure to win the soul of a horse or not? The rude authorities of these critics, after a proper legal dispute and examination of the cause, will find that his **Dark Lordship** <note>Referring to the Devil.</note> would hardly spend so much labor for a soul that is so perishable, transitory, and not immortal like that of a man—for which he gapes so greedily and thirstily.

But if they reply that the Devil does this to delude the credulous physician, and thereby prepares the means to win his soul: I respond that it is in vain to do through many things what can be done through fewer. The physician has already cured many men; would this not suffice for the Devil to get possession of him, or must he also assist him in curing brute beasts so that the obligation for the physician’s soul becomes more certain? Perhaps I would grant more faith and authority to those loud-thundering and high-minded judges (or rather, "condemners") and annihilators of the power of **Jehovah** <note>God.</note>, if they would attribute to the Devil only what belongs to him—namely, if one man could cause the loss of many souls. But who is so foolish as to boast of such things, when the Devil has surely won them on the first occasion?

From these things, therefore, any sagacious reader can most clearly discern that "Foster’s Sponge" is here squeezed dry, insofar as it is as certain as certainty itself that the natural Balsam of one animal sympathizes...

<summary>Robert Fludd argues that because humans can digest horse meat and turn it into their own blood and flesh, there must be a biological "sympathy" between the two species. He uses this to defend the Weapon Salve’s effectiveness on animals and scoffs at the idea that such healings are the work of the Devil.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s debate with William Foster. In the previous section, Fludd was arguing for the "sympathy" or natural connection between different substances that share the same "acting virtue" or "balsamic spirit." He now transitions into a specific anatomical debate regarding the origin of bones.</meta>

...to [associate] with its like in another, so far as both obtain one and the same acting virtue and one balsamic spirit in nature and condition, which is common to all specific things.

---

## CHAPTER II.
->In which it is proved, contrary to the dogma of the sponge-bearer, that human<-
->bones naturally proceed from the blood.<-

->*The Bare Assertion of Fludd’s Text.*<-

Blood is mixed with **muminia** <term>muminia: a Paracelsian term for the vital "mummy" or life-force preserved in the body</term> or flesh, fat, and **usnea** <term>usnea: specifically "usnea cranii humani," a moss that grows on the skulls of the dead, highly prized in 17th-century medicine</term> or the moss of bones, which was the beginning of blood and the nourishment of all.

### Foster’s Collection.
These ingredients have their beginning and nourishment from the blood.

->*The Action of the cleansing sponge itself.*<-

### Foster.
Secondly, I deny that human bones take their beginning and nourishment from blood: for Physicians and Philosophers claim that they have their principle from the thicker seminal parts, and their nourishment from blood or marrow, or from both.

->*Here his sponge is wrung out.*<-

### Fludd.
I truly wonder that our Censor, like a comic parasite, now denies and then in the same breath affirms the same thing: for first he denies that bones have their nourishment from blood, and then afterwards he concludes that they do. Come now, let us pass over this hesitant error and get to the point. Human bones (he says) have their principle from the thicker seminal parts: *Therefore not from blood.* This conclusion is erroneous: for if he truly wants to look at the nature of the seed [semen], he will find it to be originally nothing other than the purest part of the blood strained through two kinds of vessels: the purer and internal part of which arises from the arterial vessel; the thicker and external part from the venous vessel. 

Why does Foster need to look into the notes of Bauhin <note>Gaspard Bauhin (1560–1624), a famous Swiss botanist and anatomist</note> or the opinions of Galen, and those of many others differing from them, and thus establish his own **ipse dixit** <note>original: "ipse Dixit" — a Latin phrase meaning "he himself said it," used to describe an unproven assertion based solely on authority</note> as his total force and power, when his own eyes would teach him—if he ever knew anatomy, as perhaps his father knew—that the source of the seed is blood of two natures, namely Arterial and Venous? For the preparatory seminal vessels, which alter and purify it, have their origin and head from the great artery and the vena cava. 

This being so, I would certainly like to know from Foster whether he thinks the seed does not proceed from blood as its original source, seeing as the vessels from which it flows are filled with nothing else but blood? I care nothing for that *ipse dixit*, when the contrary appears in anyone's visual experience. For some want the substance of the seed to proceed from the brain; and others from the subtler parts of the whole body; and some want it to arise from the purer part of the four humors, which is the same as saying it proceeds from blood, which is composed of the four humors, although the Element of Air predominates. 

But it is certain that blood is its source, and that is apparent by visual demonstration. Since this is so, I ask you, my Foster, what error is there in me saying that blood is the beginning of bones, when you yourself confess that their immediate existence is from the seed, whose immediate existence is blood? Again we are taught that the seat of life is in the blood: if therefore the seed brings forth life, it receives that gift of life from the blood.

<margin>Acts 17.</margin>
Finally, it is evident from these things that the life-giving Spirit of the Lord (who is the animator of the four winds, whence the Prophet Ezekiel called Him to give life to the slain) moves and works radically in the spiritual blood, and the seed is animated and moved by this spiritual blood, which is the internal part of the seed that Philosophers call the "semen," in whose center the life-giving Spirit of the Lord works and acts; and then afterwards this Spirit in the seed forms the skin, flesh, bones, and nerves, giving them life, action, and motion. All these things are expressed by that patient man Job in this manner:
> "As milk you poured me out (that is, in the form of seed), like cheese you curdled me; you clothed me with skin and flesh, you knit me together with bones and nerves, you gave me life by your mercy, and by your visitation you preserved my spirit, but all these things you have hidden in your mind." <note>A paraphrase of Job 10:10-13, using the metaphor of cheesemaking to describe the formation of an embryo</note>

But I know all these things are from you. From which it is clear that God works all things radically by beginning in the blood: and for this reason the Apostle said, not without cause:
> "In Him (he says) we live, move, and are." <note>Acts 17:28</note>

<summary>Fludd argues against Foster's claim that bones do not originate from blood, asserting that semen is actually a refined form of blood produced by the arteries and veins. He concludes by linking the biological role of blood to the spiritual "Spirit of the Lord" which animates all life, citing Job and the Apostle Paul.</summary>

<header>FOSTER'S SPONGES.</header>

# CHAPTER III.
## In which it is proved, contrary to the opinion of our sponge-bearer, that the Spirit resides in separated blood.

### Fludd’s Bare Text.

**I**n the blood is the spirit of life, and it dwells with the radiant soul, and works in a hidden manner.

---

->Foster’s Collection.<-

->In the blood vital spirits reside; in these vital spirits is the soul, in a hidden manner.<-

---

->The act of his cleansing sponge.<-

->*Foster.*<-

Thirdly, I deny that any spirits reside in separated blood, and Casmannus <note>Otto Casmann (1562–1607), a German schoolmaster and physician who wrote on "Christian anthropology."</note> asserts this: that in parts separated from the body, no spirits remain; and he says that even the Devil himself cannot produce or preserve any in them.

---

->The wringing of the sponge.<-

->*Fludd.*<-

You see here that this novice <note>original: "tyronem," a beginner or trainee</note> has nothing to support his dogma except "he said so": for if this fails, all further expectation of proof vanishes. But let it be proven that this assertion of his and his master's is erroneous in three ways. First, by philosophical reason: for since any nature cut off from the living trunk of its multiplication is refilled with the balsamic salt of the nature of the tree or plant from which it arose, through which it exists as what it truly is, it can hardly be that it does not have some of its usual spirit of life within it, though it does not act, but rests in its center.

<margin>Wisdom 12:1</margin> Next, from the Holy Scriptures: for (as has been proved abundantly and even to the point of nausea before) shed blood and slaughtered flesh teem with living spirits, although they remain in them potentially. Otherwise, why would the Israelites be forbidden from eating fat and blood? For it is said, because the blood is the seat of the soul or the spirit of life: for, if that spirit of life had flown away from it, what sin would it have been to eat of it? But the Text says: for it is the seat of life, and therefore they are commanded to pour it out on the ground. Again, let our Foster respond to this: The incorruptible spirit of God is in all things. Therefore, it is in shed blood, flesh, fat, and bones separated from the whole. Finally, by common experience: for we find that fat, blood, and **mummy** <term>mummy: (mumia) a substance used in medicine, often made from preserved or dried human remains, believed to contain concentrated life-force</term> possess unique healing properties, which they could hardly have if all the spirits they received from the <margin>1 Cor. 22 <note>Likely a citation error in the original text; 1 Corinthians only has 16 chapters. It may refer to 1 Cor 12:22.</note> Wisdom 16:12</margin> living body had exhaled. But it is the sole office of the incorruptible spirit and the Word to heal; and therefore, since these ingredients have the property of healing, they must necessarily participate in their existence with this beneficial spirit, whose nature is to expel and remove all corruption, disease, and other natural impediments. "Your Word," says Solomon <margin>Wisdom 16</margin> "heals all things": for in it alone is life. Therefore, it is the quickening spirit. <margin>John 1</margin> Furthermore, I know and have seen with my own eyes a great abundance of spirits, which were excited from the essence of corrupted blood and fat by the activity of fire, even a small amount; so that by the natural heat of the hand they were seen to jump and dance in the air in the form of tiny atoms. This is an evident sign that the spirit of life hides in dead blood, though it appears at least potentially in the essence of the dead body with respect to us. Again, if this were not so, would it be possible for dead blood, flesh, and fat to nourish a living creature, since like is nourished by its like? This could not be, unless in blood, flesh, and fat natural and quickening spirits were duly abounding <note>original: "scerēt", likely an abbreviation for "scaterent", meaning to teem or overflow</note> to preserve their like in the living creature. And therefore, one species of flesh nourishes both man and beast, fish and bird; because all these natural spirits are of one kind and condition. Is it not, I pray, common knowledge that flesh and fat exposed to the sun are quickly converted into living worms? Which could not happen unless the spirit of life resided in the flesh and fat after the creature was dead. Indeed, I have seen an entire dead cow, which I exposed to the Sun for a certain purpose, converted entirely (except for the bones) into wormy little animals. This is a truly evident argument for the presence of the quickening spirit in dead flesh, blood, and fat. Indeed, by Hercules, I have observed that the **Balsam of Wheat** <term>Balsam of Wheat: an alchemical oil or essence extracted from wheat grains, thought to contain the plant's vital "balsam" or life-energy</term> abounds in it to such an extent that, if it is placed in rainwater, in a short interval of time it produces longish worms of a whitish color. Flesh produces the same effect after putrefaction. Wherefore it is as certain as can be that the spirit of life is in dead blood and fat; yes, and in the grain, which although it does not act unless it is excited...

<summary>Fludd argues against Foster by claiming that the "spirit of life" remains in blood and flesh even after death or separation from the body, using Scriptural prohibitions on eating blood and the spontaneous generation of worms as proof. He asserts that this latent vital spirit is what gives medicinal substances, like "mummy," their healing power.</summary>

EXPLANATION OF SECTION III. Fol. 25

is stirred up and procured through the acting property of the life-giving spirit, working in such an organic body—as in the fire of the Sun, a living creature, and similar things—nevertheless, it is most certain that this spirit remains in amputated blood, fat, flesh, and bones, etc. From these things, one may discern (friendly reader) how Casmannus <note>Otto Casmann (1562–1607), a German physician and philosopher who argued that "spirits" do not remain in separated limbs.</note> and his thorough disciple Foster have failed. But it is necessary that we excuse ourselves modestly, since it is human to err; why, I ask, should I judge these men to be more universal in science than Bernard? Yet even Bernard does not see everything. And yet, "the blind horse Bayard" <note>original: "caecus equus Bayardus." A common medieval proverb referring to someone who acts with bold ignorance, like a blind horse charging into battle.</note> or (as the English say, blind Bayards) is ready to judge and censure anyone, even though the subject is unknown to himself. Therefore, let Foster keep his authority shut up in his purse; for I certainly do not respect or value it, having natural reason, the testimony of Holy Scripture, and finally common experience and ocular demonstration to prove the contrary. 

And if indeed his master Casmannus teaches him that even the Devil himself cannot produce or preserve any spirit in those things, I wonder indeed how the Devil could perform the cure of this Weapon Salve, since the liniment does not have a spirit from itself, and yet the Devil cannot produce or preserve any in its ingredients? And if he says that the Devil is most powerful in experience and provides this effect through other herbs and simples: I would indeed wish him to tell me why herbs or other simples (since they are also merely as if dead and without spirits after they have been gathered) would, by Foster’s own rule, yield to the Devil as a medium for performing the effect of this hidden cure, and not rather those ingredients which are gathered from the human body? For human ingredients are closer and more familiar to their own species, and for that reason more benign and friendly to it than stranger medicines, such as vegetables and the like. If the reader has a mind to weigh all this properly, he will perceive that everything our "sponge-bearer" brings forth on page 8 is mere nonsense.

<margin>Page 8.</margin>
> The Devil (he says) persuades the physician that the effect is by a virtue reaching toward the wound, while he himself (skilled by long experience in all arts, and even in the medical art) secretly applies some other virtual medicine of his own, working to cure the wound, so that he may thus delude his overly-credulous wandering physicians, and persuades them that this ointment, which drips from the Executioner’s pouch, <note>Foster is mocking the salve's ingredients, which often included moss from the skull of a hanged man.</note> has performed it.

O wonderful miracle! And what does the Devil gain from it? Surely by curing a man in that way, whose body and soul are in the hands of the Almighty? In the hand 
<margin>Job 12:11</margin> 
of Jehovah (says Job) is the soul of every living thing and the spirit of all human flesh. Does he think God would lose His right through such a weak and feeble cunning? Not at all; would He give and concede His Word to the Devil—which, as Solomon says, heals all things—so that he might deceive Him of His own heritage? What? Will the Devil do good where no advantage or profit is sought for himself? And why then should it not be by the virtual contact of this medicine, as having a closer kinship with man and therefore being a readier healer than any other medicine which can work by any virtual contact? 

A truly remarkable fiction! It is as if someone wanted to persuade me that it is not the Magnet which attracts iron, but that the Devil employs some other creature to perform that task, so that he might deceive and impose upon the Philosopher or the sailor. These are only fables—inventions, I say, of a fantastic brain, which, to persuade us of his imaginary and by no means probable faith, would have us believe that castles are built in the air, and that we are deluded by the Devil in all our good actions. By flying on the wings of Foster’s wit and grasp, one must necessarily crash into the clouds of error and thus, in the fog of ignorance, forget the works of our God and Savior, and acknowledge by the palpable delusions of Mr. Foster that those things are craftily perfected by the enemy of God. And how, by God! has it happened that the Devil has become or turned out to be such a distinguished student of Physics, and so skilled a practitioner in the healing art—he who spends all his skill and has placed the best fruits of his industry in acting the part of a slaughtering cow <note>likely an idiom for a destroyer of life</note> and to destroy? Truly marvelous! Not at all; far be it: but blessed be our God, who by sending forth the kindness of His countenance alone sends health where, how, and to whom He pleases. But let me proceed.

---

->### CHAPTER IV.<-

->*This chapter shows Foster’s error when he says that the soul does not reside in the spirits according to a certain hidden manner.*<-

->*The second movement of the sponge against the same Text.*<-

->*Foster*<-

**F**ourthly, I deny that the soul resides in the spirits in any hidden way. The Stoics <note>Ancient Greek school of philosophy.</note> indeed thought that the spirits were the bonds of the soul and body. But the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle.</note> and the Theologians judge this to be superfluous, since the body is generated for the sake of the soul, and the soul is created for the sake of the body, and both constitute the whole composite. What need is there for those bonds to tie them together? There is a reciprocal desire and appetite for coming together at the first threshold, and an effort after union to cling together. The soul can in no way depend upon or reside in its organs, but the spirit resides in the soul, etc.

# FOSTER’S SPONGES.

### We wring out once again, in this argument, this swollen and bulging sponge in this manner.

#### Fludd.

Even if I have sufficiently responded to that point in the preceding sections, it is nonetheless necessary that I wring the sponge <note>Fludd uses "sponge" as a metaphor for Foster’s book, which he is "wringing out" to extract the truth.</note> a little more forcefully; otherwise, it will retain some of the juice of truth in its porous paunch or belly.

<margin>Psalm 19. Wisdom 12:1.</margin>
I have said before that the seat of the soul is in the blood, and its principal vehicle is the **radical moisture** <note>The "humidum radicale," a vital fluid in medieval medicine thought to be the fuel of life.</note>, just as we see that the spirit of life in the macrocosm <note>The "great world" or the universe.</note> has placed its Tabernacle in the celestial sun; and again it is said, the incorruptible spirit is in all things; but this is that life-giving spirit which quickens all things, and therefore resides in the blood and consequently in the spirits contained within it, in a secret and mystical way.

As for the opinion of the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle’s philosophy, who dominated university learning at the time.</note> and certain theologians who consider it superfluous for there to be a bond between the body and the soul: that doctrine, by Hercules, is quite full of error and false. Firstly, because the soul and body are so opposed to each other in their complexion that, unless a union occurs between the two extremes, it would be impossible for them to run together at the same time; or if they could or should meet each other, nevertheless the pure and celestial light of the soul would suddenly abandon the impure and earthy darkness of the body. For how can two contraries agree in one? Do we not see that all influences from above require an airy chariot, a vehicle or medium, so that they may be led into bodies and united with one another? Why did God designate and place the air between heaven and earth, if not to be in the place of a vehicle for uniting celestial things with terrestrial ones, as if by a *bond of love*? Can we have a better proof from these things in the "typical world" <note>The physical world seen as a reflection or "type" of divine truth.</note> than that Archetypal one? Is not the Father united to the Son through the Holy Spirit, which St. Augustine and many others call the **divine bond of love**? Therefore, according to the Archetypal image, all things were created, both in the macrocosm and in the microcosm <note>The "little world," referring to man as a reflection of the universe.</note>:

<margin>Psalm 33.</margin>
For the Prophet says, *by the word of the Lord the heavens were formed, and all their virtues by the breath of his mouth*; so that the virtuous union and coupling, which is established between the effects of the word—in which there is life—and the creature to be quickened, is that beneficial and incorruptible spirit, by which bond God holds his essential relation to the creature. Through

<margin>Hebrews 1. Job 26:9. Job 28:25.</margin>
this spirit, all discordant elements are gathered and combined in a union and amicable consent, whence it is called peace, love, and concord; which (as the Apostle says) carries and sustains all things by the word of his power: Through it, weight and proportion are assigned to the air, and the clouds are bound together, or suspended in measure, and the waters are gathered so tenaciously in thick clouds that they are not divided. Finally, in the macrocosm, heaven and earth are stabilized and connected by the word of God, as St. Peter reports, otherwise the elements

<margin>2 Peter 3.</margin>
would be perpetually in conflict: and by the same reasoning, the soul and body, or the heaven and earth in the microcosm, are bound together by this immediate and eternal bond; otherwise body and soul would never cohabit together, but would wage war against each other, since they are as contrary in nature as fire and water. But unless the spirit of the air were placed between these contrary elements to bind them together, they would never agree, nor persist in their sphere: no more would the soul and body without a spiritual medium. As we see, then, the heaven of the microcosm is composed of light and spirit in proportion, and as if glued together by the eternal spirit, which is the infusion of life in both: so also the spirit in man is so firmly united to the soul by the spiritual word—which is the nexus, coupling, or glue of life—that it is not possible to separate one from the other, unless it be by that spirit which joins them together. And this can easily be gathered from these words of the Apostle: *The word of*

<margin>Hebrews 4.</margin>
*God is living, and more piercing than any two-edged sword, reaching even to the division of soul and spirit.* By which it is argued that life consists of soul and spirit, and these two are so united to each other by the **Bond of the Word**, that no one, except the composer or uniter, can perform that separation from them. And for this reason, we may see that the bond is most firm both between spirit and soul, and between soul and body: and therefore, as the soul is worthier than the spirit, so the Spirit excels the body in dignity; and consequently, the spirit is placed in a certain proportion between the soul and body, no differently than air between the sun and the earth. Wherefore it is the greatest absurdity among the Peripatetics to deny that bond and union, and even more absurd for Foster, who excuses himself so lightly, saying that blood was procreated for the sake of the soul, and the soul for the sake of the body, and therefore there is no need for bonds to tie them together. A most wretched conclusion, I say, for a Philosopher and Theosophist <note>A student of "Theosophy," which at this time meant the study of divine wisdom through nature and scripture.</note> so prominently appearing; as if he were to say that two extremes could be joined together more easily without a medium or intervening bond, or an intermediate spirit to join and unite them, than the two ends of a diameter in a circle without an intermediate point or center. And it is more absurd in him to say that there can be a reciprocal appetite of two extremes and opposites to meet and cohabit in a contrary way for the first time, when they are so opposed to each other that the wise man says: *The body, liable to corruption, weighs down the*

<margin>Wisdom 9.</margin>
*soul, and the earthly dwelling depresses the mind full of many cares.* Is it not strange,

<summary>Robert Fludd argues that the soul and body are polar opposites that require a spiritual "bond" or intermediary—analogous to air between heaven and earth—to remain united. He critiques William Foster and Aristotelian philosophers for failing to recognize this essential tripartite structure of life.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s rebuttal against William Foster. Fludd is currently arguing that the soul is distinct from the body and requires a mediating "spirit" to connect them, contrary to Foster's simpler Aristotelian view.</meta>

<header>EXPRESSION OF PART III.</header>

Is it not consistent with nature that any captive spirit would yearn for its freedom, especially a radiant soul <note>original: "lucidam animam"</note> that is imprisoned in a dark, bodily dungeon? For this very reason, Iamblichus <note>A Neoplatonic philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) who wrote on the soul's descent into matter.</note> says that the soul sleeps within the human body. Porphyry <note>Another Neoplatonic philosopher (c. 234–305 AD) and student of Plotinus.</note> holds that the soul is always "sick" while in the body. And Hermes Trismegistus <note>The legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the Hermetic corpus is attributed; a foundational source for Fludd’s alchemy.</note> claims the body is a "veil of ignorance" for the soul. From these sources, it is certain that there is a spirit which detains the soul in that dark prison. From these points, one can see how "excellent" Foster's doctrine truly is! But, to patch up his argument, he proceeds in this manner: *and they strive after union to cling together so,* etc. This is true if he is speaking on behalf of the dark body, which unwillingly deserts and leaves the radiant soul, which is its treasure. But regarding the soul, we see how many there are who, to avoid the shackles of this prison, pour out their own blood or lay hands on themselves and destroy themselves. We find many, both among the elect (as we find in the Scriptures) and among the common worldly folk, who earnestly seek and strive with God—being weary of this worldly life—to be dissolved and to depart from this life. "I desire to be dissolved and to be with Christ," <margin>Philippians 1:23.</margin> says a certain person, etc. From this, we gather that the soul does not desire to live in the body or with the body, as Master Foster concludes. And when the soul departs, it cannot leave its body without the spirit, so firm is their union, as the Apostle shows in the text cited before. Nor can the spirit entirely abandon that relationship which it has to the body, as has been said. I conclude, therefore, simply against Foster’s assertion, that the soul depends on and rests upon the spirit by a certain tight union, and in a reciprocal way, the spirit rests upon the soul. It is no different than how an **agent** <note>The "agent" is the active force; the "patient" is the thing receiving the action.</note> cannot be considered an agent without a **patient**, nor a patient without its agent. And therefore it is necessary that both of these be united in one; and consequently, just as an essential agent acts from the center to the circumference, in the same way, it must be conceived that the active soul is contained within the spirits, like an agent in a patient, or a soul in a body, or lightning in a cloud.

And thus far we have proceeded in wringing out and squeezing every bit of validity from Foster's "Sponge" regarding this business. I come now to the following.

# CHAPTER V.

->*The author’s essential carrier of sympathetic virtue, in this sponge-<-
->*bearing chapter, offers our man a "straw hospitality," because<-
->*he calls him* **Tom Long** <note>A traditional English nickname for a slow or tedious carrier/messenger.</note> *the carrier; read and<-
->*you will perceive the manner of it.*<-

---

->### The Plain Assertion of Fludd’s Text.<-

From these things it is clear that this spiritual line, invisibly drawn out and extended in the air between the location of the wounded man and the medicine box <note>original: "myrothecium," a container for ointments or oils.</note>, carries with it its animal form. This soul or spirit of life can no more be divided from its whole or entirety contained in the body of the wounded man than solar rays can be divided from the Sun. Therefore, just as solar rays floating in the world-spirit are like a messenger or courier between heaven and earth, in the same way, this "animal ray" is a faithful conductor of healing nature from the medicine box to the wounded body. And this medium or directing and carrying line—namely, that which conducts and transfers that health-bringing spirit by means of the soul or spirit of life—is that spirit which is invisibly extended or drawn out in the air. If it had not been shaped and formed in a certain occult manner, the virtue of the ointment would evaporate or be poured out this way or that, and thus bring no benefit to the wounded man.

---

->### Foster's Collection.<-

| Summary of Foster | Fludd's Reply |
|-------------------|---------------|
| The spirit of the shed blood is carried through the air (which is the bearer of the spirit of every thing) to its body. And this spirit, by stretching through the air in a straight invisible line, transfers the healing power from the anointed weapons to the wounded man. For the weapons communicate that power to the blood clinging to them, the blood to the spirits, and the spirits, conducted through the air, communicate it to the body; and thus the patient (without the application of a plaster) is naturally healed, etc. | Firstly, I make no mention of a "straight and extended line," but I speak only of the transfer and direction of vital spirits from the wounded body to the medicine box, and then afterward of the **Magnetic attraction** of the healing virtue, backward along a certain invisible line drawn out in the air. Then he says, wandering from my text, that the weapons communicate the virtue of the ointment to the blood clinging to them; but I never said nor understood any such thing. For a closer kinship exists between the ointment and the blood than between the weapons and the ointment. But I value him and his efforts so little <note>literally "as a lock of wool" (tam flocci)</note> that I am willing to leave him to his own freedom of will. |

<header>Fludd.</header>

It can be clearly and evidently discerned here how he corrupts my text so that it fits his own purpose: For

# THE FOSTERIAN SPONGE.
The action of the cleansing sponge upon this.
<meta>catchword: Text</meta>

->**Foster.**<-

**F**ifthly, I deny the Doctor's Porter <note>original: "Bajulum," meaning a carrier or porter</note>—namely, his direct invisible line carrying healing power through so many miles from the weapon-salve to the wound. This is surely that "Tom Long the carrier" <note>An English idiom for a notoriously slow messenger</note>, who never wants to carry out his order. But the Sun, with its rays, is the true carrier between heaven and earth; and thus [he compares] this ointment between the weapon and the wound (O incomparable comparison!). The Sun is called *Sol* as if it were *solus* <note>Latin for "alone." This was a common medieval etymology.</note>, as having no equal, no creature operating similarly to itself. But the Doctor, like another Archimedes, thinks he can perform a similar operation by emitting rays like the Sun.

->**Fludd.**<-

And even if you call this Messenger of mine that "Tom Long the carrier," nevertheless his mummy <term>Mumia: In Paracelsian medicine, this refers to the vital life-force or "balsam" contained within the blood and flesh, which Fludd believes can act across distances.</term> will execute its task so promptly and certainly, and return so suddenly to you (who are its slanderer), carried on the swift wings of truth, that by the conclusion of this Text, it shall provide you with nothing but straw-like hospitality and fellowship as your reward. 

I do not say, my good man, that just as a solar ray is the true messenger between heaven and earth, so the Ointment is the messenger between the weapon and the wound; (O the wonderful capacity of so learned a man, who imagines in his own fantasy things that do not exist!). Rather, I say that just as the solar ray is the messenger between heaven and earth, so there is a ray of the life-giving and incorruptible spirit in the internal man—which is his own "heaven"—connecting to the blood that lies hidden in the Ointment. 

This is no different than how a grain of wheat in the honest and fertile earth receives life-giving comfort from the Solar rays. After the grain decays <note>Fludd refers to the alchemical idea that a seed must "die" or rot to release its life-force.</note>, it attracts its tiny soul (now set free) upward toward the source of life from which it descended the previous year, using a certain Magnetic virtue to multiply the grain. But, because it is hindered by its Elemental body, it remains fluttering here and there in the air; and by drawing more of its own kind from on high, it is multiplied from one grain into many. 

Is it therefore impossible that a similar thing could occur between the ray proceeding from the body and the blood corrupted in the Ointment? Could not a tiny atom of life, rising through the decay of the dead blood and without the hindrance of its oily, drained earth, rise little by little toward the source of life itself? But because all of this is expressed in vivid colors in my response to that very objection—made in the first and second chapters of the second member of this treatise—I refer the reader to those places. There, he will find every part of that imperfect refutation answered, his "sponge" completely wrung out, and all the venom of bitterness he directed against my text utterly erased and annihilated.

Then he proceeds thus: *The Sun is called "Sol" as if it were "alone," as having no equal, no creature operating similarly to itself; but the Doctor acts like another Archimedes, etc.* My dear Priest, I always make an exception for Plato: <margin>Ecclesiastes 1.</margin> that incorruptible spirit which (as the son of Sirach says) was created before all things, must necessarily be excepted. Do you notice this, my lord? For I told you that your "sponge," in its inquiry into this Text, would find nothing but straw-like fellowship. 

Indeed, I do not hope that you would prefer the visible Sun over this divine spirit which gives the Sun its glory and activity. What? The creature over the Creator? Matter over form? The passive over the active? Is this the "divinity" of our Priest? Or is it only Philosophy that teaches him? No matter what Cicero <note>original: "Tullius"</note> tells us—that the Sun is truly the only actor in the world—<margin>Psalm 19.</margin> it is certain that it was this Spirit who "placed his Tabernacle in the sun," and through that spirit alone the Sun lives, moves, and operates, both here below and there above. <margin>Acts 17.</margin> And it is one and the same spirit that imparts the spirit of life to all creatures, and consequently to us men, "by which we live, move, and have our being." 

It is He who, in turn, placed His Tabernacle <margin>1 Corinthians 6:19.</margin> in man just as in the sun. And therefore, <margin>1 Corinthians 12:27.</margin> we are called "members of Christ" and "members of the Holy Spirit." By this, Foster’s wisdom and sagacity—indeed, that of a Christian Theologian—can be properly distinguished and discerned when he says in this Text: *But Dr. Fludd, like another Archimedes, knows how to perform an operation by emitting rays similar to it, etc.* Not at all! Let them not so boldly ascribe to me what belongs only to God my Creator, even though our Foster wished to ascribe it to the Devil.

As for the full response to his refutation, I refer you (as I said) to the second part of this treatise. I shall now proceed to that "tenth wave" <note>original: "decumanum," referring to the largest wave in a series</note> of an assault, in which his sponge rubs my text quite vigorously. But it profits him no more than those who try to wash an Ethiopian white; it will, I hope, be seen as utterly vain labor.

---

### CHAPTER VI.

*Here, contrary to the intention of our "Cabalist Sponge" [Foster], it is proved, first: that evil spirits can contaminate and alter the airy spirit of man into their own nature; also, that Devils have airy bodies granted to them in their creation. Finally, the mutability and inconsistency of the Refuter in his principal argument is revealed.*

Fol. 27
EXPRESSION OF SECTION III. <note>original: "EXPRESSIO MEMB. III." Referring to a specific part of his argument.</note>
G 3
<meta>catchword: spirit-</meta>

### Fludd’s Text.

From this, therefore, arises that secret combination and union which is formed between an evil spirit and evil sorcerers <term>Cacomagos: From the Greek 'kakos' (evil) and 'magos' (mage); refers to practitioners of black magic.</term> or witches, by which fools and dullards are foully deceived by the Devil. Thus the Devil, or evil spirit, through the enticement of this reward, fulfills the will or desire of the witch. Furthermore, a pact is struck between them: specifically, that the spirit, in whatever form, may daily suck a portion of blood. By this, the spirit lurking in the blood of the mage becomes of one nature and condition with the evil spirit. In this manner, the person's own spirit is converted into a diabolical condition, making it impossible for them to depart from the worship and veneration of the Devil.

---

### Foster’s Collection from the Text.

That there is such a sympathy between blood in the body and blood drawn and sucked out of the body is as manifest as can be, as seen in the example of witches. The Devil sucks the blood from them; the blood remaining with the Devil participates in his evil nature, and having a path back through its spirits to the body of the witch, it causes all her blood to sympathize with that which the Devil possesses. Thus, the blood changes the nature of the sorceress, and so they turn out evil and diabolical.

<margin>Fludd’s note</margin>
Here again, one may see that he twists my text beyond the true intent of my meaning. But I shall grant him his desire and humor the good man for his own honor's sake.

---

### The action of his cleansing sponge upon this text.

->FOSTER.<-

The Doctor proves this by the example of blood sucked from witches by the Devil; blood which, remaining with the Devil and sympathizing with the blood in the bodies of the witches, changes their nature and renders them evil and diabolical. O, what a profound example! Here the Doctor gathers and builds the foundation of his argument, which neither true Philosophy nor orthodox Theology will allow us to accept. Blood of a witch remaining with a blood-sucking Devil sympathizes with the blood of the witch's body? How can this be? How can blood, a bodily substance, remain with the Devil and be incorporated into him? etc.

---

### Here the validity of that sponge is wrenched away.

->FLUDD.<-

O, the wondrous wit of our sponge-bearer! O, the "spongy" light and intellect of such a difficult concept! But, by Hercules, if the Angels (as he says) were incorporeal, how could the food and drink—bodily substances—remain with the Angels whom Abraham received at his feast? <note>Referring to Genesis 18, where Abraham hosts three celestial visitors.</note> If they were incorporeal, or if they took on accidental bodies, could they eat and drink naturally? Or was Abraham so lacking in his senses that he would offer food and drink to deceptive figures and masked ghosts? A man so deep in divine mysteries would surely not be so sluggish as to offer them his nourishment if he had known they did not naturally consume it. Almost the same absurdity could be attributed to Lot. It is indeed beyond the reach of worldly men to examine this doubt correctly, or to skillfully open and unlock it. 

But suppose it is granted that Angels and Devils are not corporeal, but spiritual creatures; yet he confesses elsewhere that the Devil can put on and assimilate into himself an organic body, such as that of a man, a dog, or a cat; and consequently, those Angels put on human forms. I ask you: when a syringe, a pipe, or a suction cup draws something in, is it the tool that acts, or is it the spirit within the tool that draws? A man does not work by his body, but by his internal spirit. Nor does blood do anything of itself, but by the hidden life-giving spirit that acts within it. The human throat and tongue serve as the organs of the voice, but it is the spirit that acts. If the Devil enters a body, such as that of a pig or a man, did he not make use of the creature's organic voice and make such men speak to Christ? But it was a spiritual act of the Devil, who mixed himself with the airy spirit of the possessed beast or man, and caused it to respond according to the will of the actor. 

Thus, I say, he sucked the blood by the aid of the mouth and spirit of the creature he put on; not that the blood in its gross nature remained with the Devil in his spiritual substance. Rather, I say, the spiritual substance within the blood, which participates in the air, is easily suited to join and form a union through contact with that of the Devil. Just as we see Amber, when it is burned, emit—

<summary>Fludd defends his theory of blood-sympathy by arguing that spirits (Angels or Demons) can interact with physical matter like blood by inhabiting organic forms or mixing with the spiritual essence found within the blood.</summary>
